\displaystyle\leq{\delta^{\prime}}\sqrt[4]{\frac{{9^{t}}B^{2}}{\delta^{\prime}}}+2(\delta^{\prime})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[f^{\prime}_{x,low}(\mathbf{w})^{2}]

which implies that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[f^{\prime}_{x,low}(\mathbf{w})^{2}]\leq 2\left(\sqrt[4]{{9^{t}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}B^{2}}+2(\delta^{\prime})^{2}\right)

Thus, if
B>2\sqrt[4]{{9^{t}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}B^{2}}+4(\delta^{\prime})^{2}
then we can improve our bound on
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\in S^{d-1}}[f^{\prime}_{x,low}(\mathbf{w})^{2}]
. Letting
B^{*}
be the infimum of the bounds we can obtain, we must have that
B^{*}=2\sqrt[4]{{9^{t}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}(B^{*})^{2}}+4(\delta^{\prime})^{2}
. Solving for
B^{*}
gives

\sqrt{B^{*}}=\sqrt[4]{C{(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}}+\sqrt{\sqrt{{9^{t}}(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}+4(\delta^{\prime})^{2}}\leq\max{\{3\sqrt[4]{{9^{t}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}},3{\delta}^{\prime}\}}\leq 3\sqrt[4]{{9^{t}}{(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}}

where the first inequality follows by considering whether
4(\delta^{\prime})^{2}\leq 3\sqrt{{9^{t}}(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}
or
4(\delta^{\prime})^{2}>3\sqrt{{9^{t}}(\delta^{\prime})^{3}}
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[f^{\prime}_{x,low}(\mathbf{w})^{2}]\leq 10\sqrt{{9^{t}}{\delta}^{3}}
which implies that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x,low})(\mathbf{w})^{2}]\leq 10\sqrt{{9^{t}}{\delta}^{3}}
.

Since
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(f(\mathbf{w})-(1-2\delta^{\prime}))^{2}]=4(1-\delta^{\prime}){\delta^{\prime}}^{2}+{\delta^{\prime}}(-2+2\delta^{\prime})^{2}\leq 4{\delta^{\prime}}
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[f^{\prime}_{x,high}(\mathbf{w})^{2}]\leq 4{\delta^{\prime}}
which implies that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x,high})(\mathbf{w})^{2}]\leq 4{\rho_{x}^{2t}}{\delta}
. The result now follows as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x})(\mathbf{w})^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x,low})(\mathbf{w})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x,high})(\mathbf{w})^{2}]

\displaystyle{\leq 10(3^{t})\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}+4{\rho_{x}^{2t}}\delta.}

∎

We now show that the proportion of monochromatic triples must be at least
\frac{\delta}{8}
. As we observed earlier, in order to avoid having
\frac{\delta}{8}
proportion of monochromatic triples, we must have that
(N_{\rho_{x}}f_{x})(-\mathbf{w})<\frac{1}{4}
for at least
\frac{\delta}{2}
proportion of
\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}
. Since
N_{\rho_{x}}f_{x}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[f_{x}(\mathbf{w})]+N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x}\geq 1-2\delta+N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x}
, we have that
|(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x})(\mathbf{w})|\geq\frac{3}{4}-4\delta
for at least
\frac{\delta}{2}
proportion of
\mathbf{w}\in S^{d-1}
. Since
\delta\leq\frac{1}{16}
, this implies that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x})(\mathbf{w})^{2}]\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\cdot(\frac{1}{2})^{2}=\frac{\delta}{8}
.

However, Lemma
3.13
implies that if
\delta>0
is sufficiently small then
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x})]\leq\frac{\delta}{10}
. To see this, take
t=50
and observe that since each
\rho
that appears is at most
.9
,
\rho^{2t}\leq\frac{1}{80}
. Since
\delta<\frac{1}{40000(9^{t})}
,
10(3^{t})\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq\frac{\delta}{20}
. By Lemma
3.13
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}}[(N_{\rho_{x}}f^{\prime}_{x})(\mathbf{w})^{2}]\leq 10(3^{t})\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}+4{\rho^{2t}}\delta\leq\frac{\delta}{20}+\frac{\delta}{20}=\frac{\delta}{10}.

This gives a contradiction so the proportion of monochromatic triples must be at least
\frac{\delta}{8}
, as needed.
∎

4
Improved Analysis for
\operatorname{MAJ}
Polymorphism

In this section, we prove that the robust approximate algorithm for 2-SAT of Charikar–Makarychev–Makarychev
[
CMM09
]
has the same
f(\epsilon)=O(\sqrt{\epsilon})
loss guarantee for any PCSP template with the
\operatorname{MAJ}
polymorphism family. This improves over the analysis of Brakensiek–Guruswami–Sandeep
[
BGS25
]
, which only proved
f(\epsilon)=\widetilde{O}(\sqrt[3]{\epsilon})
loss. Furthermore, in the next section, we shall extend the result (with a slightly worse loss guarantee) to PCSP templates on non-Boolean domains with more general types of polymorphisms.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a promise template with
\operatorname{MAJ}\subseteq\operatorname{Pol}(\Gamma)
. Then,
\PCSP(\Gamma)
has a uniform robust algorithm with loss function
f(\epsilon)=O_{\Gamma}(\sqrt{\epsilon}).

4.1
CMM Algorithm

We state the algorithm of Charikar–Makarychev–Makarychev (henceforth “CMM”) as follows.

•

Input:
\epsilon>0
, weighted instance of
\PCSP(\Gamma)
on variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and clauses
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
as in
Section

2.1
.

•

Solve the basic SDP to find a vector solution
\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with objective value at least
1-\epsilon
.

•

Sample a random Gaussian
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{N}(0_{n+1},I_{n+1})
.

•

For all
i\in[n]
, round
x_{i}
to
+1
if
\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{0}+\mathbf{r}\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle\geq 0
and
-1
otherwise.

We call this the
\epsilon
-CMM algorithm to make the choice of
\epsilon
explicit. We make use of the following structural lemma proved by
[
BGS25
]
.

Lemma 4.2

(Lemma 4.5 of
[
BGS25
]
, adapted)
.

Let
P\subseteq Q\subseteq\{-1,1\}^{k}
be such that
\operatorname{MAJ}\subseteq\operatorname{Pol}(P,Q)
. For any
\mathbf{b}\not\in Q
, there exists
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with the following properties.

•

\|\mathbf{w}\|_{1}=1
.

•

For all
i\in[k]
,
b_{i}w_{i}\leq 0
.

•

For all
\mathbf{a}\in P
,
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle\geq 0
.

We note that this lemma is proven in much more generality in
Proposition

5.6
.
As a corollary, we have that an approximate version of Lemma
4.2
holds for approximate solutions to the SDP.

Lemma 4.3

(Implicit in
[
BGS25
]
)
.

Let
P\subseteq Q\subseteq\{-1,1\}^{k}
be such that
\operatorname{MAJ}\subseteq\operatorname{Pol}(P,Q)
. Fix
\mathbf{b}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}\setminus Q
and let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
be as guaranteed by Lemma
4.2
. Then, for any
1-\gamma
approximate vector assignment
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k})
to
P
we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\geq-\gamma.

(7)

Proof.

Since
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k})
is a
1-\gamma
approximate vector assignment to
P
, there exists a probability distribution
\mu
over
\{-1,1\}^{k}
such that
\mu(P)\geq 1-\gamma
and for all
i\in[k]
,

\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}a_{i}\mu(\mathbf{a}).

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}a_{i}\mu(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle\mu(\mathbf{a}).

By Lemma
4.2
,
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{a}\in P
. Furthermore,
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle\geq-1
for all
\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}
since
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{1}=1
. Thus, (
7
) follows since
\mu(\{-1,1\}^{k}\setminus P)\leq\gamma
.
∎

We now state our main technical result.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
P\subseteq Q\subseteq\{-1,1\}^{k}
with
\operatorname{MAJ}\subseteq\operatorname{Pol}(P,Q)
. Let
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k})
be a
1-\gamma
approximate vector assignment to
P
. For any
\mathbf{b}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}\setminus Q
, the probability that the
\epsilon
-CMM algorithm when run on
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k})
outputs
\mathbf{b}
is at most

\displaystyle 600k\frac{\max(\epsilon,\gamma)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}

(8)

From this, Theorem
4.1
easily follows.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Our Basic SDP solution
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{n})
has the property that each clause
C_{j}
is
1-\gamma_{j}
approximately satisfied where
\mathbb{E}_{j}[\gamma_{j}]\leq\epsilon
.
Let
k_{j}
denote the arity of the clause
C_{j}
, and define
k_{\max}=\max_{j}(k_{j})
.
By Theorem
4.4
, the probability that
C_{j}
is assigned some specific
assignment which fails weakly satisfy
C_{j}

is at most

\displaystyle 600k_{j}\frac{\max(\epsilon,\gamma_{j})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

Therefore, by the union bound, the probability that
C_{j}
is weakly satisfied is at least

1-2^{k_{j}}\left(600k_{j}\frac{\max(\epsilon,\gamma_{j})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\geq 1-2^{k_{j}}\left(600k_{j}\frac{\epsilon+\gamma_{j}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\geq 1-2^{k_{\max}}\left(600k_{\max}\frac{\epsilon+\gamma_{j}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right).

Thus, the expected fraction of clauses that are weakly satisfied is at least

1-1200\cdot 2^{k_{\max}}k_{\max}\sqrt{\epsilon},

as desired. ∎

4.2
Proof of Theorem
4.4

We seek to bound the probability that

\displaystyle b_{i}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle\geq 0,\quad\forall i\in[k].

(9)

Let
\mathbf{w}
correspond to
\mathbf{b}
as in Lemma
4.2
. Let
\mathbf{u}:=w_{1}\mathbf{v}_{1}+\cdots+w_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}
. By Lemma
4.3
, we have that
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\geq-\gamma
. Furthermore, since
b_{i}w_{i}\leq 0
for all
i\in[k]
, we have that (
9
) holding implies that

\displaystyle\langle w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle\leq 0,\quad\forall i\in[k].

(10)

Summing over all
i\in[k]
, this implies that.

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle\leq 0.

(11)

To work toward this, we first show that
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle
cannot stray too much from
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle
.

Lemma 4.5
.

\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle+2\gamma
.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be the probability distribution over
\{-1,1\}^{k}
with
\mu(P)\geq 1-\gamma
that corresponds to
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k})
. Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.3
that

\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle\mu(\mathbf{a}).

Likewise, we can compute that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{i}w_{j}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{i}w_{j}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}a_{i}a_{j}\mu(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle^{2}\mu(\mathbf{a}).

Thus,

\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle(\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle-1)\mu(\mathbf{a}).

Recall that
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{1}=1
.
For any
\mathbf{a}\in P
, we have that
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle\in[0,1]
, so
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle(\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle-1)\leq 0
. Furthermore, for any
\mathbf{a}\in\{-1,1\}^{k}
,
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle\in[-1,1]
, which implies that
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle(\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}\rangle-1)\leq 2
. Thus,
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\leq 2\gamma
.
∎

For each
i\in[k]
, let
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{=}
be the component of
\mathbf{v}_{i}
parallel to
\mathbf{u}
, and let
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}
be the component of
\mathbf{v}_{i}
perpendicular to
\mathbf{u}
. That is,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{i}^{=}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{u}\rangle}{\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle}\mathbf{u},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{v}_{i}-\frac{\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{u}\rangle}{\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle}\mathbf{u}.

Let
\eta:=\max(\gamma,\epsilon)
. We now split into cases.

Case 1,
\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}\geq 8\eta\ln(1/\epsilon)
.

In that case, we have that
\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}\geq 4\gamma
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.5
,

\langle\mathbf{u},\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}-2\gamma)\geq\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

Therefore, (
11
) holds only if

\langle\mathbf{r},-\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}\rangle>\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

Note that the LHS is normally distributed with mean 0 and variance 1. Therefore, by Proposition
2.2
, we deduce that (
9
) holds with probability at most

\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)^{2}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{\eta}{\epsilon}\log(1/\epsilon)\right)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

Case 2, there exists
i\in[k]
such that
\|w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\|\geq 1/(10k)
.

First, note that by Proposition
2.2
, for all
\delta\geq 0
we have that

\displaystyle\Pr[\delta\leq\left|\langle\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}\rangle\right|\leq 2\delta]=\Pr\left[\frac{\delta}{||\mathbf{u}||}\leq\left|\langle\mathbf{r},\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{2\delta}{||\mathbf{u}||}\right]\leq 2\frac{\delta}{\|u\|\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{{\delta}^{2}}{2\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}}}\leq\frac{\delta}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}e^{-\frac{{\delta}^{2}}{2\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}}}.

(12)

Assume that (
9
) holds. Then, by (
10
), (
11
), and Lemma
4.5
, we have that

\displaystyle 0\geq\left\langle w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{0}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right\rangle\geq\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\geq\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}-2\gamma)\geq\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\gamma.

(13)

Now consider the probability distribution of the random variable
\langle w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{0}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\rangle
conditioned on the value of
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle
. Observe that this is a Gaussian distribution with standard deviation
\|{w_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\|\geq\frac{1}{10k}
(and arbitrary mean).
By Proposition
2.2
, the probability that (
13
) happens is at most

10k(|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle|+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\gamma)\leq 10k\left(2\delta+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\gamma\right).

Thus, the probability that both
\delta\leq\left|\langle\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}\rangle\right|\leq 2\delta
and (
9
) hold is at most

\frac{\delta}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}e^{-\frac{{\delta}^{2}}{2\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}}}\cdot 10k\left(2\delta+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\gamma)\right)\leq\frac{40k{\delta}e^{-\frac{{\delta}^{2}}{2||\mathbf{u}||^{2}}}}{||\mathbf{u}||}\max\left\{\delta,\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right\}.

Call the RHS of the above expression
f(\delta)
. We now split our analysis into subcases based on the size of
\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}
.

Case 2a,
\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}\leq 4\eta
.

We seek to bound
\sum_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}f(2^{i}\|\mathbf{u}\|)
. If
i\leq 1
, note that

f(2^{i}\|\mathbf{u}\|)\leq\frac{40k\cdot 2^{i}\|\mathbf{u}\|}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}\max\left\{2^{i}\|\mathbf{u}\|,\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right\}\leq 40k\cdot 2^{i}\cdot\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

Summing over all
i\leq 1
, we obtain a contribution of at most
160k\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon}
. Otherwise, if
i\geq 2
, we have that

f(2^{i}\|\mathbf{u}\|)\leq 40k\cdot 2^{i}\cdot e^{-2^{2i-1}}\cdot\max\left\{2^{i+1}\sqrt{\eta},\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\frac{160k\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\cdot 2^{2i-1}e^{-2^{2i-1}}.

Using that the function
x\mapsto x\cdot e^{-x}
is nonincreasing in
[1,\infty)
, we find

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}2^{2i-1}e^{-2^{2i-1}}\leq\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}ie^{-i}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}x\cdot e^{-x}\,dx=1

and, thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}f(2^{i}\|\mathbf{u}\|)\leq\frac{160k\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

As a consequence, the total probability bound is at most
\frac{320k\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}
.

Case 2b,
\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}>4\eta
.

By logic similar to Case 1, we have that (
9
) holds only if
\langle\mathbf{r},-\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}\rangle>\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}.
Thus, we only need to consider
\delta\geq\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}
. Consider any such
\delta=\lambda\|\mathbf{u}\|\geq\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}/(2\sqrt{\epsilon})
so
2\lambda\sqrt{\epsilon}\geq\|\mathbf{u}\|
. We have that

f(\lambda\|\mathbf{u}\|)=40k\lambda e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}}\max{\left\{\lambda\|\mathbf{u}\|,\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right\}}\leq 160k\lambda^{3}e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}}\sqrt{\epsilon}.

Since
2\lambda\sqrt{\epsilon}\geq\|\mathbf{u}\|>\sqrt{4\eta}
, we have that
\lambda>1
. Thus, the relevant probability of (
9
) occurring is at most

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}160k\cdot 2^{3i}\cdot e^{-2^{2i-1}}\sqrt{\epsilon}\leq 600k\sqrt{\epsilon}\leq 600k\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon},

as desired.

Case 3,
\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}<8\eta\ln\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)
and there is no
i
such that
\|{w_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\|\geq\frac{1}{10k}
.

In this case, since
\mathbf{u}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\|{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}_{i}}\|}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{|{w_{i}}|\|{\mathbf{v}_{i}}\|}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{|{w_{i}}|}=1
, we have that

1=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{||{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}_{i}}||}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}{||{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}^{=}_{i}}||}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}{||{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}^{\perp}_{i}}||}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}{||{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}^{=}_{i}}||}+\frac{1}{10},

and

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}{{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}^{=}_{i}}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}{{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}^{\perp}_{i}}}\right\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}\leq 8{\eta}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right).

Putting these observations together, there must be an
i
such that
{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}_{i}}=c\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}+{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}^{\perp}_{i}}
where
|c|\geq\frac{1}{2k}
. We now define
t,x,y
as follows

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\langle{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}_{i}},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle}{c\sqrt{\epsilon}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle:=\langle\frac{\mathbf{u}}{||\mathbf{u}||},\mathbf{r}\rangle,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{i}{\langle{w_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle}.

Note that
t
is a constant, but
x
and
y
are random variables depending on
\mathbf{r}
. In particular,
x
is a Gaussian random variable with mean zero and variable one. Furthermore, note that

\langle{w_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle=c(x-t)+\langle{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}_{i}}^{\perp},\mathbf{r}\rangle.

We next observe the following. First, in order for (
9
) to hold, we must have that
-y\leq\langle{w_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle\leq 0
which implies that

\displaystyle|\langle{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}_{i}}^{\perp},\mathbf{r}\rangle+c(x-t)|\leq y.

(14)

Second, since
-y=\sum_{i}{\langle{w_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle}=\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\geq\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(||\mathbf{u}||^{2}-2\gamma)=||\mathbf{u}||x+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(||\mathbf{u}||^{2}-2\gamma)
, we have

\displaystyle|x|\geq-x\geq\frac{||\mathbf{u}||}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}-\frac{2\gamma}{||\mathbf{u}||\sqrt{\epsilon}}+\frac{y}{||\mathbf{u}||}.

(15)

To finish the proof, we split into cases based on the values of
(y,c(x-t)).

Case 3a,
y\leq 4\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}
.

Since
|c|\geq\frac{1}{2k}
, but
\|w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\|\leq\frac{1}{10k}
, we have that
\langle{w_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{v}_{0}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\rangle=c(x-t)+\langle{w_{i}}{\mathbf{v}_{i}}^{\perp},\mathbf{r}\rangle
has standard deviation at least
|c|/2\geq\frac{1}{4k}
. Thus, by Proposition
2.2

\Pr\left[|\langle w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp},\mathbf{r}\rangle+c(x-t)|\leq 4\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right]\leq\frac{4\eta}{|c|\sqrt{\epsilon}/2}\leq 16k\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

That is, if we condition on
y\leq\frac{4\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}
, the probability that (
14
) holds (and thus the probability that (
9
) holds) is at most
16k\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}
.

Case 3b, there is
\delta\geq 4\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon}
such that
y\geq\delta
and
|c(x-t)|\leq 4\delta
.

Note that
\delta/2\geq 2\gamma/\sqrt{\epsilon}
, so by (
15
), we have that

\displaystyle|x|\geq\frac{||\mathbf{u}||}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}+\frac{\delta}{2||\mathbf{u}||}.

(16)

Thus, by the Proposition
2.2
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pr[|c(x-t)|\leq 4\delta\wedge y\geq\delta]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[|c(x-t)|\leq 4\delta\wedge(\ref{eq:cond-x'})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8\delta}{|c|}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}+\frac{\delta}{2\|\mathbf{u}\|}\right)^{2}}\leq\frac{8\delta}{|c|}e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\leq 16k{\delta}e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}}.

Case 3c, there is
\delta^{\prime}\geq 4\delta\geq 16\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon}
such that
y\in[\delta,2\delta]
and
|c(x-t)|\in[\delta^{\prime},2\delta^{\prime}]
.

Note that (
16
) still holds, so we have that

\displaystyle\Pr[|c(x-t)|\in[\delta^{\prime},2\delta^{\prime}]\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[|c(x-t)|\in[\delta^{\prime},2\delta^{\prime}]\wedge(\ref{eq:cond-x'})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta^{\prime}}{|c|}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{u}\|}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}+\frac{\delta}{2\|\mathbf{u}\|}\right)^{2}}\leq\frac{2\delta^{\prime}}{|c|}e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}}.

Let
d=\|w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp}\|
. We then have by Proposition
2.2
that

\displaystyle\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})\mid|c(x-t)|\in[\delta^{\prime},2\delta^{\prime}]\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[(\ref{eq:cond-y})\mid|c(x-t)|\in[\delta^{\prime},2\delta^{\prime}]\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\delta}{d}e^{-\frac{\delta^{\prime 2}}{8d^{2}}},

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
\langle w_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\perp},\mathbf{r}\rangle
must land in an interval of length at most
2\delta
with minimum values of
\delta^{\prime}-2\delta\geq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}
. Therefore, combining the previous two calculations, we have that

\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})\wedge|c(x-t)|\in[\delta^{\prime},2\delta^{\prime}]\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]\leq\frac{8\delta\delta^{\prime}}{|c|d}e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}-\frac{\delta^{\prime 2}}{8d^{2}}}.

Finishing Case 3.

We now put these subcases together. First, condition on
\delta\geq 4\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon}
. We have by cases 3b and 3c that

\displaystyle\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[|c(x-t)|\in[\delta^{\prime},2\delta^{\prime}]\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})\mid|c(x-t)|\in[2^{i}\delta,2^{i+1}\delta]\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]

\displaystyle\leq 6k{\delta}e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}}+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{2^{i+3}\delta^{2}}{|c|d}e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}-\frac{2^{2i}\delta^{2}}{8d^{2}}}

\displaystyle=6k{\delta}e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}}+16k\cdot\delta e^{-\delta/\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{2^{i}\delta}{d}e^{-\frac{2^{2i}\delta^{2}}{8d^{2}}}.

Now,

\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{2^{i}\delta}{d}e^{-\frac{2^{2i}\delta^{2}}{8d^{2}}}\leq\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}2\int_{2^{i-1}}^{2^{i}}\frac{\delta}{d}e^{-\frac{s^{2}\delta^{2}}{8d^{2}}}\,ds\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\delta}{d}e^{-\frac{s^{2}\delta^{2}}{8d^{2}}}\,ds=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{8}}\,ds=2\sqrt{2\pi}\leq\frac{11}{2},

so

\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})\wedge y\in[\delta,2\delta]]\leq 100k\delta e^{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}}.

We next incorporate the bound from case 3a to get that

\displaystyle\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})\wedge y\leq 4\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon}]+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\Pr[(\ref{eq:MAJ-bad})\wedge y\in[2^{i}\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon},2^{i+1}\eta/\sqrt{\epsilon}]]

\displaystyle\leq 16k\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}+100k\cdot\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}2^{i}e^{-\frac{2^{i}\eta}{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq 16k\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}+100k\cdot\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}2^{i}e^{-2^{i}}

\displaystyle\leq 50k\cdot\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}},

as desired. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.4
and thus also of Theorem
4.1
.

5
Rounding schemes for separable PCSPs

The goal of this section is to extend the results of Section
4
to the case of promise CSPs on non-Boolean domain. Throughout this section, we focus on promise CSPs such that the domains of the weak and strong constraint languages coincide.

We begin with two definitions.
Given a tuple
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
, we denote by
\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times A}
the one-hot encoding of
\mathbf{a}
; i.e., the
(i,a)
-th entry of the matrix
\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}
is
1
if
a_{i}=a
,
0
otherwise. Also, we let
{\langle-,-\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}
denote the Frobenius inner product of matrices, given by
{\langle M,N\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}=\Tr(M^{\top}N)
.

Definition 5.1
.

Fix a PCSP template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and a function
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{A}\to A
. We say that
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
\rho
-separable if
A=B
and, for each promise relation
(P,Q)
of some arity
k
and each
\mathbf{b}\in A^{k}\setminus Q
, there exists a matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times A}
such that

1.

\|W\|_{1}=1
;

2.

W\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}
;

3.

{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\geq 0
for each
\mathbf{a}\in P
; and

4.

{\langle W,M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\leq 0
for each
M\in\rho^{-1}(\mathbf{b})
.

In part 4. of the above definition, the expression
\rho^{-1}(\mathbf{b})
indicates the set of
k\times A
real matrices whose
i
-th row is in
\rho^{-1}(b_{i})
for each
i\in[k]
.

Intuitively, in
Definition

5.1
,
\rho
should be thought of as an arbitrary rounding scheme, and the idea is to consider polymorphism families that are compatible with the scheme in the sense that they can be used to avoid the occurrence of large integrality gaps.
We will also assume that the rounding function
\rho
is well-behaved in the following weak sense.

Definition 5.2
.

Fix some parameter
\alpha>0
. We say that a function
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{A}\to A
is
\alpha
-conservative if the inequality

\displaystyle q_{\rho(\mathbf{q})}\geq\alpha\sum_{b\in A}q_{b}

holds for each
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
with
A=B
be a
\rho
-separable PCSP template for an
\alpha
-conservative function
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{A}\to A
. Then
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is robustly solvable via SDP with loss
{O}_{\alpha,A}(\sqrt{\epsilon}\log(1/\epsilon))
.

Remark 5.4
.

We point out that the conditions imposed on
\rho
in
Definition

5.1
alone are too broad to ensure robust solvability with the loss guarantee of
Theorem

5.3
, as they are met by CSP templates such as Horn-SAT which (assuming UGC) lack a
\epsilon^{O(1)}
-robust rounding scheme. Hence, the
\alpha
-conservativity of
\rho
is needed.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we shall consider the case in which
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
contains a single promise relation
(P,Q)
of some arity
k
. Let
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\{\mathbf{v}_{1,a}:a\in A\},\ldots,\{\mathbf{v}_{k,a}:a\in A\})
be a
(1-\gamma)
-approximate vector assignment to
P
for some
\gamma>0
, as per
Section

2.2
.
Fix
\epsilon>0
, and run the
\epsilon
-CMM algorithm on such vector assignment, using
\rho
as the rounding function (see
Section

4.1
). More precisely, we apply the following procedure:

1.

Sample a random Gaussian
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{N}(0_{n},I_{n})
(where
n
is the dimension of the space where the SDP vectors live).

2.

Define
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{0}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}
, and let
\mathbf{c}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
be the vector whose
a
-th entry is
c^{(i)}_{a}=\langle\mathbf{v}_{i,a},\mathbf{z}\rangle
for each
i\in[k]
.

3.

Let
b_{i}=\rho(\mathbf{c}^{(i)})
for each
i\in[k]
.

Consider now the vector
\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in A^{k}
, and suppose that
\mathbf{b}\not\in Q
. Take a corresponding matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{k,A}
as guaranteed from the fact that
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
\rho
-separable, and define the vector

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a}\mathbf{v}_{i,a}.

Consider also the matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times A}
whose
(i,a)
-th entry is
c^{(i)}_{a}
. Observe that
M\in\rho^{-1}(\mathbf{b})
, so part
4.
of Definition
5.1
guarantees that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}\rangle={\langle W,M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\leq 0.

(17)

Observe now that, for each
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
, it holds that

\displaystyle{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}=\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i,a_{i}}.

Hence, letting
\mu
denote a probability distribution corresponding to the vector assignment, we find

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,i^{\prime}\in[k]}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in A}w_{i,a}w_{i^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i,a},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle=\sum_{i,i^{\prime}\in[k]}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in A}w_{i,a}w_{i^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}\\
a_{i}=a\\
a_{i^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mu(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}\mu(\mathbf{a})\sum_{i,i^{\prime}\in[k]}w_{i,a_{i}}w_{i^{\prime},a_{i^{\prime}}}=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}\mu(\mathbf{a}){\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}^{2}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i,a},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle=\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}\\
a_{i}=a\end{subarray}}\mu(\mathbf{a})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}\mu(\mathbf{a})\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i,a_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}\mu(\mathbf{a}){\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}.

(18)

Now, part
1.
of Definition
5.1
implies that, for each
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
,

\displaystyle|{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}|=|\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i,a_{i}}|\leq\sum_{i\in[k],a\in A}|w_{i,a}|=\|W\|_{1}=1,

while part
3.
implies that
0\leq{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}
for each
\mathbf{a}\in P
.
Hence, the quantity
{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}^{2}-{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}
is at most
2
for every
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
, and it is nonpositive if
\mathbf{a}\in P
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}\mu(\mathbf{a})({\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}^{2}-{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}})\leq 2\gamma

(19)

(where we have used that the vectors
\mathbf{v}_{0},\{\mathbf{v}_{i,a}\}
form a
1-\gamma
approximate vector assignment to
P
).

Take
\delta=2\max(\epsilon,\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)
and
\eta=\sqrt{2(\delta+2\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)}+\frac{\delta+\gamma}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}
.
Suppose first that
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle>\delta
.
Note that the random variable
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{0}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\rangle
is distributed as a Gaussian with mean
\mu=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle
and standard deviation
\sigma=\|\mathbf{u}\|
. Applying part (b) of
Proposition

2.1
and observing that
\gamma\leq\frac{\delta}{2}
, we deduce that the probability that (
17
) happens is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle^{2}}{2\epsilon\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle^{2}}{2\epsilon(\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle+2\gamma)}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2\epsilon(\delta+2\gamma)}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\delta}{4\epsilon}\right)\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}.

In the expression above,
the second inequality uses the fact that the function
x\mapsto\frac{x^{2}}{x+c}
is increasing if
x,c\geq 0
, while the last inequality holds since

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta}{4\epsilon}=-\frac{2\max(\epsilon,\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)}{4\epsilon}=\frac{\max(\epsilon,\gamma)\log\epsilon}{2\epsilon}\leq\frac{\log\epsilon}{2}=\log(\sqrt{\epsilon}).

Thus, the probability that
\mathbf{b}\not\in Q
is at most
\sqrt{\epsilon}
in this case.

Suppose now that
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\leq\delta
. Using (
19
), we deduce that, in this case,
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\rangle\leq 2\gamma+\delta
.
Recall that
\mathbf{r}
is a random Gaussian sampled from the distribution
\mathcal{N}(0_{n},I_{n})
, so
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle
is distributed as a Gaussian with mean
0
and standard deviation
\|\mathbf{u}\|
.
Using part
(b)
of
Proposition

2.1
, we find that

\displaystyle\Pr\left(|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle|\geq\sqrt{2(\delta+2\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2(\delta+2\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)}{2\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\log(1/\epsilon)\right)=2\epsilon.

(20)

Observe now that
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\geq-\gamma
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}\mu(\mathbf{a}){\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\geq\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}\setminus P}\mu(\mathbf{a}){\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\geq-\left\lVert W\right\rVert_{1}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}\setminus P}\mu(\mathbf{a})\geq-\gamma,

where the first equality is (
5
), while the first and last inequalities come from part 3. and part 1. of
Definition

5.1
, respectively.
In particular, this implies that
|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle|\leq\max(\delta,\gamma)<\delta+\gamma
.
Therefore, with probability at least
1-2\epsilon
by (
20
) it holds that

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}\rangle|=|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle|\leq|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{r}\rangle|+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle|\leq\sqrt{2(\delta+2\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)}+\frac{\delta+\gamma}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}=\eta.

(21)

Consider now, for each
i\in[k]
,
the sets
A_{i,+}=\{a\in A:w_{i,a}>0\}
and
A_{i,-}=\{a\in A:w_{i,a}\leq 0\}
, and let
c_{i}=\sum_{a\in A}|w_{i,a}|
.
Using part 2 of Definition
5.1
,
we find that
\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a}=0
, so

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A_{i,+}}w_{i,a}=-\sum_{a\in A_{i,-}}w_{i,a}.

Fix
i\in[k]
and take some
\lambda>0
. Consider the
k\times A
matrix
M^{(i,\lambda)}
having
(i,b_{i})
-th entry
1
,
(j,b_{j})
-th entry
\lambda
for each
j\neq i\in[k]
, and all other entries
0
. Recall that the function
\rho
is assumed to be
\alpha
-conservative for some
\alpha>0
. It follows that
M^{(i,\lambda)}\in\rho^{-1}(\mathbf{b})
, so
{\langle W,M^{(i,\lambda)}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\leq 0
by part 4. of Definition
5.1
. Since this holds for each positive
\lambda
, we deduce that
w_{i,b_{i}}\leq 0
, so
b_{i}\in A_{i,-}
for each
i\in[k]
.

Let now
\beta=\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},\frac{1}{2|A|})
.
Consider the matrix
N^{(i,\lambda)}
defined by
N^{(i,\lambda)}_{i,b_{i}}=1-\beta|A_{i,+}|
,
N^{(i,\lambda)}_{i,a}=\beta
for each
a\in A_{i,+}
,
N^{(i,\lambda)}_{i,a}=0
for each
a\in A_{i,-}\setminus\{b_{i}\}
,
N^{(i,\lambda)}_{j,b_{j}}=\lambda
for each
j\neq i\in[k]
, and
N^{(i,\lambda)}_{j,a}=0
elsewhere.
Using again the
\alpha
-conservativity of
\rho
, we find that
N^{(i,\lambda)}\in\rho^{-1}(\mathbf{b})
and, thus,
{\langle W,N^{(i,\lambda)}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\leq 0
. Taking the limit as
\lambda\to 0
, this means that
\beta\sum_{a\in A_{i,+}}w_{i,a}\leq(\beta|A_{i,+}|-1)w_{i,b_{i}}
.
Therefore, for each
i\in[k]
, we have

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}|w_{i,a}|=\sum_{a\in A_{i,+}}w_{i,a}-\sum_{a\in A_{i,-}}w_{i,a}=2\sum_{a\in A_{i,+}}w_{i,a}\leq 2\frac{\beta|A_{i,+}|-1}{\beta}w_{i,b_{i}}

\displaystyle=2\frac{1-\beta|A_{i,+}|}{\beta}|w_{i,b_{i}}|.

(22)

The
\alpha
-conservativity of
\rho
also guarantees that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}},\mathbf{z}\rangle=c^{(i)}_{b_{i}}\geq\alpha\sum_{a\in A}c^{(i)}_{a}=\alpha\sum_{a\in A}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i,a},\mathbf{z}\rangle=\alpha\langle\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{z}\rangle.

(23)

Using
Proposition

2.3
, we find that

\displaystyle\Pr(\|\mathbf{r}\|>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}})\leq O(\exp(-1/\sqrt{\epsilon})).

Hence, from Cauchy–Schwarz, we deduce that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{z}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{0}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{r}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\geq-|\langle\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{r}\rangle|+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\geq-\|\mathbf{r}\|+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\geq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}

(24)

with probability
1-O(\exp(-1/\sqrt{\epsilon}))
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}},\mathbf{z}\rangle\leq\|\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{z}\|=\|\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{r}+\frac{\mathbf{v}_{0}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\|\leq\|\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}}\|\cdot(\|\mathbf{r}\|+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}})\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\|\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}}\|

(25)

with probability
1-O(\exp(-1/\sqrt{\epsilon}))
.
Combining (
23
), (
24
), and (
25
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}}\|\geq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2}\langle\mathbf{v}_{i,b_{i}},\mathbf{z}\rangle\geq\frac{\alpha\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2}\langle\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{z}\rangle\geq\frac{\alpha}{4}

(26)

with probability
1-O(\exp(-1/\sqrt{\epsilon}))
.

Let us now define
\mathbf{y}_{i}=\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a}\mathbf{v}_{i,a}
for each
i\in[k]
.
Observe that
\beta|A_{i,+}|\leq\beta|A|\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
Combining (
5
) with (
26
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|=\sqrt{\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a}^{2}\|\mathbf{v}_{i,a}\|^{2}}\geq|w_{i,b_{i}}|\|\mathbf{v}_{i,\mathbf{b}_{i}}\|\geq\frac{\beta c_{i}}{2(1-\beta|A_{i,+}|)}\cdot\frac{\alpha}{4}=\frac{\alpha\beta c_{i}}{8(1-\beta|A_{i,+}|)}\geq\frac{\alpha\beta c_{i}}{8}

with probability
1-O(\exp(-1/\sqrt{\epsilon}))
.
Note now that

\displaystyle\Pr\left(|\langle\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{z}\rangle|<\frac{8}{\alpha\beta}\eta\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|\right)\leq\frac{\frac{16}{\alpha\beta}\eta\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}{2\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}=\frac{8}{\alpha\beta}\eta

by part
(a)
of
Proposition

2.1
.
Using part
4.
of Definition
5.1
in a similar fashion as before, we find that
\langle\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{z}\rangle\leq 0
; thus,

\displaystyle\Pr\left(\langle\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{z}\rangle<-\frac{8}{\alpha\beta}\eta\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|\right)\geq 1-\frac{8}{\alpha\beta}\eta.

As a consequence, by the union bound, the property

\langle\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{z}\rangle<-\frac{8}{\alpha\beta}\eta\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|\quad\mbox{for each}\quad i\in[k]

holds with probability
1-O_{\alpha,A}(\eta)
.
Observe now that

\displaystyle\eta\geq\frac{\delta+\gamma}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\geq\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\geq\frac{2\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\geq\sqrt{\epsilon}\geq\exp(-1/\sqrt{\epsilon})

for
\epsilon
small enough.
Hence,
putting it all together, it follows that, with probability
1-O_{\alpha,A}(\eta)
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{z}\rangle=\sum_{i\in[k]}\langle\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{z}\rangle<-\frac{8}{\alpha\beta}\eta\sum_{i\in[k]}\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|\leq-\eta\sum_{i\in[k]}c_{i}=-\eta\|W\|_{1}=-\eta,

thus contradicting (
21
). It follows that, in this case,
\mathbf{b}\in Q
with probability
1-O_{\alpha,A}(\eta)
.

Finally, recall that
\eta=\sqrt{2(\delta+2\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)}+\frac{\delta+\gamma}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}
, where
\delta=2\max(\epsilon,\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)
and
\gamma
is the error of our vector assignment for a single clause
(P,Q)
. Since our instance is assumed to be
(1-\epsilon)
-satisfiable, we know that the average value of
\gamma
across all clauses is at most
\epsilon
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\gamma]\leq\epsilon
, where the expectation is taken over the clauses of the instance of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. As such,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\delta]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[2(\epsilon+\gamma)\log(1/\epsilon)]=2(\epsilon+\mathbb{E}[\gamma])\log(1/\epsilon)\leq 4\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2(\mathbb{E}[\delta]+2\mathbb{E}[\gamma])\log(1/\epsilon)}+\frac{\mathbb{E}[\delta]+\mathbb{E}[\gamma]}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}

(Jensen’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2(4\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)+2\epsilon)\log(1/\epsilon)}+\frac{4\epsilon\log(1/\epsilon)+\epsilon}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq 10\sqrt{\epsilon}{\log(1/\epsilon)}.

In summary, whether
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle>\delta
or
\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle\leq\delta
, the loss of SDP is at most
O_{\alpha,A}(\sqrt{\epsilon}\log(1/\epsilon))
, as required.
∎

It is easy to check that a Boolean PCSP admitting majority polymorphisms of all (odd) arities satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
5.3
.
(Note, however, that the loss in the theorem above is slightly worse—by a
\log(1/\epsilon)
factor—than the
{O}(\sqrt{\epsilon})
loss we managed to obtain in the Boolean majority case, see Theorem
4.1
.)
More interestingly, Theorem
5.3
can be applied to other classes of PCSPs, whose robust solvability was not known.
This is done by relating the polymorphism families defining such PCSPs to corresponding rounding functions as per Definition
5.1
.
In particular, we now show that
any PCSP containing
plurality
polymorphisms of all arities satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
5.3
and, thus, is robustly solvable.

Definition 5.5

(
[
BWŽ21
]
)
.

A function
f:A^{n}\to A
is a plurality if

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{a})=\argmax_{a\in A}\{\#\mbox{ of occurrences of $a$ in $\mathbf{a}$}\}

for all
\mathbf{a}\in A^{n}
,
with ties broken in a way that
f
is symmetric.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
with
A=B
be a PCSP template such that
\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
contains plurality polymorphisms of all arities. Then
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is robustly solvable by SDP with loss
{O}(\sqrt{\epsilon}\log(1/\epsilon))
.

Proof.

Consider any function
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{A}\to A
satisfying
q_{\rho(\mathbf{q})}\geq q_{a}
for each
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
,
a\in A
(i.e.,
\rho
is the
\argmax
function, with ties broken arbitrarily). Note that
\rho
is
\alpha
-conservative for
\alpha=\frac{1}{|A|}
. We claim that
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
\rho
-separable.

Fix a promise relation
(P,Q)
of
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
of arity
k
, and choose some tuple
\mathbf{b}\in A^{k}\setminus Q
.
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k\times A}
be the convex hull of the set
\{\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{a}\in P\}
. Let also
L
be the subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k\times A}
for which
\mathbf{b}
is the vector of (strict) plurality coordinates. That is,
M\in L
if and only if, for each
i\in[k]
and each
a\in A\setminus\{b_{i}\}
, it holds that
M_{i,b_{i}}>M_{i,a}
. Note that
K
is closed and
L
is open, and they are both convex sets. We claim that they are disjoint.
Suppose by contradiction that
M\in K\cap L
. Since
M
is constrained by a system of inequalities with rational coefficients, we may assume there exists a family
\{\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{a}\in P\}
of
rational
nonnegative weights summing up to
1
such that
M=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in P}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}
. Take a positive integer
n
such that
n\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
is an integer for each
\mathbf{a}
, and choose a plurality polymorphism
f\in\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
of arity
n
. Let
\textbf{c}\in A^{k}
be the tuple obtained by applying
f
coordinatewise to a list of tuples
\mathbf{a}^{(1)},\mathbf{a}^{(2)},\dots,\mathbf{a}^{(n)}\in P
, where each
\mathbf{a}
appears
n\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}
-many times. Since
f
is a polymorphism,
\mathbf{c}\in Q
. On the other hand,
M\in L
implies that
M_{i,b_{i}}>M_{i,a}
for each
i\in[k]
and each
b_{i}\neq a\in A
. By definition of plurality, we deduce that
\textbf{c}=\mathbf{b}\not\in Q
, a contradiction. It follows that
K\cap L=\emptyset
, as claimed.

Using well-known results on hyperplane separation of convex bodies
[
BV04
]
, since
\mathbb{R}^{k\times A}
is finite dimensional, there exists a linear operator
W\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times A}
and a scalar
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
such that for every
M\in K
it holds that
{\langle W,M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\geq\mu
, and for every
M\in L
it holds that
{\langle W,M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}<\mu
.
We claim that
W
is our desired matrix of weights as required by Definition
5.1
.

First,
observe that, since
L
is an open cone, we have that
{\langle W,M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}<\mu
for all
M\in L
if and only if
{\langle W,\lambda M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}<\mu
for all
M\in L
and
\lambda>0
. By sending
\lambda\to\infty
, we have that
{\langle W,M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}<0
for all
M\in L
and the RHS of this inequality cannot be made any smaller. In other words, we can assume without loss of generality that
\mu=0
.
Then, we may also assume without loss of generality that the
1
-norm of
W
is
1
, by simply rescaling it if necessary. Hence,
1.
holds.
The condition

{\langle W,M\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\geq 0
for all
M\in K
is equivalent to
{\langle W,\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{a}\in P
, so
3.
holds.
To prove
4.
, just observe that
\rho^{-1}(\mathbf{b})
is a subset of the topological closure of
L
. Hence,
4.
follows from the continuity of the map
\bullet\mapsto{\langle W,\bullet\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}
.

In order to prove condition
2.
, choose
i\in[k]
. For each
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
, consider the following matrices:

•

D^{(\alpha)}
is the
k\times k
diagonal matrix whose
i
-th diagonal entry is
1
and whose
j
-th diagonal entry is
\alpha
for each
j\neq i
;

•

J
is the
k\times A
all-one matrix;

•

M^{(\alpha,\beta)}=D^{(\alpha)}(J+\beta\Pi_{\mathbf{b}})
;

•

N^{(\alpha,\beta)}=D^{(\alpha)}(-J+\beta\Pi_{\mathbf{b}})
.

Observe that
\rho(M^{(\alpha,\beta)})=\rho(N^{(\alpha,\beta)})=\mathbf{b}
for each
\alpha>0
,
\beta>0
. Hence, by condition
4.
, it holds that
{\langle W,M^{(\alpha,\beta)}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\leq 0
and
{\langle W,N^{(\alpha,\beta)}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}\leq 0
. Using the continuity of the Frobenius inner product, we deduce that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq{\langle W,M^{(0,0)}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}=\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad 0\geq{\langle W,N^{(0,0)}\rangle}_{\operatorname{F}}=-\sum_{a\in A}w_{i,a},

thus proving that
W\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}
, as required.

In summary, we have shown that
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
\rho
-separable for a
\frac{1}{|A|}
-conservative function
\rho
. Hence, the result follows from Theorem
5.3
.
∎

6
Equality Preserves Robustness

Given a domain
A
, we define
\operatorname{EQ}_{A}:=\{(a,a):a\in A\}
. Likewise, for a domain pair
(A,B)
, we define
\operatorname{EQ}_{A,B}
to be the promise relation
(\operatorname{EQ}_{A},\operatorname{EQ}_{B})
. As an informal shorthand, given a promise template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, we let
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})+\operatorname{EQ}
denote the promise template with
\operatorname{EQ}_{A,B}
added.
Fix parameters
0<\alpha<\beta<1
. We say that
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-
robust
if there exists a polynomial-time algorithm such that when the input is an instance on variable set
V
such that there is a map
g:V\to A
that strongly satisfies at least
1-\alpha
fraction of the constraints, the algorithm outputs a map
g^{\prime}:V\to B
that weakly satisfies at least
1-\beta
fraction of the constraints.

The main goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume UGC. If
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust for some
0<\alpha<\beta<1
, then
\PCSP((\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})+\operatorname{EQ})
is
(\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}},\beta_{\operatorname{EQ}})
-robust for

\displaystyle\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathbf{A}}(\alpha^{2}\beta^{4}),

(27)

\displaystyle\beta_{\operatorname{EQ}}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta).

(28)

The assumption of UGC is required to tie the existence of robust algorithms to integrality ratios of the basic SDP, see Theorem
6.4
. One could make Theorem
6.1
unconditional by redefining robustness in terms of basic SDP integrality gaps, but we omit such a reformulation in our presentation.

It directly follows from the result above that the existence of a
minion homomorphism
16
16

16

For the definition of minions and their homomorphisms, we refer the reader to
[
BBKO21
]
.

\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\to\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
between the polymorphism sets of two PCSP templates preserves robust solvability under UGC.

Corollary 6.2
.

Consider a function
f:(0,1)\to(0,1)
with
f(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Under UGC, if
\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\to\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
and
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
f
-robust, then
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
is
g
-robust, where
g(\epsilon)=O_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}}(f(\epsilon^{1/6}))
.

Proof.

We start by introducing some terminology from algebraic PCSP theory, following
[
BG21a
,
BBKO21
]
.
Fix a relational signature
\sigma
(i.e., a finite set of relation symbols
R_{1},R_{2},\dots
, each with an integer arity
r_{1},r_{2},\dots
). A
pp-formula
over
\sigma
is a formal expression
\psi
consisting of an existentially quantified conjunction of predicates of the form
(i)
“
x=y
”, or
(ii)
“
(x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{r}})\in R
” for some
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, where
x,y,x_{i_{1}},\dots,x_{i_{r}}
are variables.
Let
k
be the number of free (i.e., unquantified) variables in
\psi
.
Given a
\sigma
-structure
\mathbf{A}
, the interpretation of
\psi
in
\mathbf{A}
is the set
\psi(\mathbf{A})\subseteq A^{k}
containing all tuples
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in A^{k}
that satisfy
\psi
, where each symbol
R
appearing in
\psi
is interpreted in
\mathbf{A}
. Let now
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
be a
\sigma^{\prime}
-structure for some possibly different signature
\sigma^{\prime}
, such that
A^{\prime}=A
. We say that
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
is
pp-definable
from
\mathbf{A}
if for
each symbol
S\in\sigma^{\prime}
it holds that
S^{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}=\psi_{S}(\mathbf{A})
for some pp-formula
\psi_{S}
over
\sigma
. Suppose now that
A^{\prime}=A^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
let
\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})
be the structure with domain
A
and relations defined as follows: For each
r
-ary symbol
S\in\sigma^{\prime}
,
\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})
has an
rn
-ary relation containing the tuple
(b^{(1)}_{1},\dots,b^{(1)}_{n},\dots,b^{(r)}_{1},\dots,b^{(r)}_{n})
for each tuple
(\mathbf{b}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{b}^{(r)})\in S^{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}
.
We say that
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
is an
n
-fold
pp-power
of
\mathbf{A}
if
\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})
is pp-definable from
\mathbf{A}
.

Take now
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{C}
, and
\mathbf{D}
as in the statement of the corollary, let
\sigma
be the signature of
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
, and let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the signature of
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}
.
It was shown in
[
BBKO21
, Theorem 4.12]
that the existence of a minion homomorphism
\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\to\operatorname{Pol}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
is equivalent to the existence of two
\sigma^{\prime}
-structures
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
such that
(i)

\mathbf{C}\to\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\to\mathbf{D}
, and
(ii)

\tilde{\mathbf{A}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
are pp-powers of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, respectively, for the same power
n\in\mathbb{N}
and via the same pp-formulae.

Recall that we are assuming that
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
f
-robust. Using
Theorem

6.1
we have that
\PCSP((\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})+\operatorname{EQ})
is
g
-robust for
g(\epsilon)=f(\epsilon^{1/6})
.
Using
[
BGS25
, Proposition 2.10]
, we deduce that
\PCSP(\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}),\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}))
is
O_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\tilde{\mathbf{A}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}(1)\cdot g
-robust.
Consider now the (standard) reduction from
\PCSP(\tilde{\mathbf{A}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
to
\PCSP(\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}),\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}))
defined as follows. For an instance
\mathbf{X}
of
\PCSP(\tilde{\mathbf{A}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
, output an instance
\hat{\mathbf{X}}
of
\PCSP(\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}),\operatorname{vec}_{n}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}))
whose domain contains
n
copies
x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}
of each
x\in X
, and whose relations
R^{\hat{\mathbf{X}}}
contain the tuple
(x^{(1)}_{1},\dots,x^{(n)}_{1},\dots,x^{(1)}_{r},\dots,x^{(n)}_{r})
for each tuple
(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
in
R^{\mathbf{X}}
. Clearly, such reduction preserves robust solvability with the same loss, so we deduce that
\PCSP(\tilde{\mathbf{A}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
is
O_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\tilde{\mathbf{A}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}}}(1)\cdot g
-robust. Finally, since
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
is a homomorphic relaxation of
(\tilde{\mathbf{A}},\tilde{\mathbf{B}})
, we conclude that the same holds for
\PCSP(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
, as required.
∎

As discussed in the introduction,
Corollary

6.2
partially confirms
1.4
due to Barto and Kozik
[
BK16
]
.
The proof of
Theorem

6.1
is rather intricate as it involves substantial modifications to the algorithm of Brown-Cohen and Raghavendra
[
BR16
]
, which itself is a modified version of Raghavendra’s algorithm
[
Rag08
]
. We begin this section with an overview of Brown-Cohen and Raghavendra’s algorithm (henceforth called the
BCR algorithm
).

6.1
An Overview of the Algorithm of Brown-Cohen and Raghavendra

In this section, we describe the BCR algorithm and its main correctness guarantee. We begin with the necessary preliminaries.

6.1.1
BCR Preliminaries

A key ingredient in the BCR algorithm is a notion of
approximate polymorphisms
. For a promise template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and a positive integer
R
, consider a probability distribution
\mathcal{P}
of maps
p:A^{R}\to B
.

Definition 6.3

(Approximate Polymorphism
[
BR16
]
)
.

We say that
\mathcal{P}
is a
(c,s)
-approximate polymorphism of
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
if for all (normalized) weighted instances
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
on variable set
X
, given
R
assignments
a_{1},\ldots,a_{R}:X\to A
each with value at least
c
, then

\mathbb{E}_{p\sim\mathcal{P}}[\operatorname{val}_{\mathcal{X}}(p\circ(a_{1},\ldots,a_{R}))]\geq s,

where for all
x\in X
, we have that
(p\circ(a_{1},\ldots,a_{R}))(x)=p(a_{1}(x),\ldots,a_{R}(x))
.

We also define
\mathcal{P}
to be an
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust polymorphism
if
\mathcal{P}
is a
(1-\alpha,1-\beta)
-approximate polymorphism. Observe that there are many trivial approximate polymorphisms with good parameters, such as probability distributions supported only on dictators. To identify the “meaningful” approximate polymorphisms, BCR use a notion of
quasirandomness
to ensure that the robust/approximate polymorphism is sufficiently nontrivial.

We start by recalling some standard Fourier-analytic notions. Fix a probability distribution
\mu\in\Delta_{A}
, and let
\{\chi_{0}=1,\dots,\chi_{|A|-1}\}
be an orthonormal basis for the vector space
L_{2}(A,\mu)
. Any function
f:A^{R}\to\mathbb{R}
can be written as
f=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{R}}\hat{f_{\sigma}}\chi_{\sigma}
, where
\chi_{\sigma}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{R}\chi_{\sigma_{j}}(x_{j})
, and the
\hat{f}_{\sigma}
are the Fourier coefficients of
f
.
Let
|\sigma|
denote the number of non-zero coordinates of
\sigma
.
The
degree
d
influence
of the
i
-th coordinate of
f
(under the probability measure
\mu
) is defined as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Inf}_{i,\mu}^{<d}(f)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{R}\\
\sigma_{i}\neq 0\\
|\sigma|<d\end{subarray}}\hat{f}_{\sigma}^{2}.

We say that an approximate polymorphism
\mathcal{P}
is
(\tau,d)
-quasirandom
if, for every probability measure
\mu\in\Delta_{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p\sim\mathcal{P}}\left[\max_{i\in[R]}\operatorname{Inf}_{i,\mu}^{<d}(p)\right]\leq\tau.

In order to describe the BCR algorithm, we shall also need to use
noise operators
. Given a parameter
\rho\in[0,1]
, we let
T_{\rho}
be the operator defined as follows:
for each function
f:A^{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle T_{\rho}f(x)=\mathbb{E}_{y\sim_{\rho}x}[f(y)],

where the expression “
y\sim_{\rho}x
” denotes that, for each
i\in[R]
,
y_{i}
equals
x_{i}
with probability
\rho
, and
y_{i}
is independently sampled according to
\mu
with probability
1-\rho
. It follows from this definition that the Fourier expansion of the operator is given by
T_{\rho}f=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{R}}\hat{f}_{\sigma}\rho^{|\sigma|}\chi_{\sigma}
.

6.1.2
The BCR Algorithm

The main result of Brown-Cohen and Raghavendra
[
BR16
]
is as follows.

Theorem 6.4
.

For any PCSP template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and any
\theta>0
, there exists
\tau>0
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for each
0<\alpha<\beta<1
, if
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
admits an
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust
(\tau,d)
-quasirandom polymorphism, then the BCR algorithm is
(\alpha-\theta,\beta+\theta)
-robust for
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
.
Conversely, assuming UGC, if
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
has any
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust algorithm, then for any
\theta>0
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
admits an
(\alpha-\theta,\beta+\theta)
-robust algorithm via the aforementioned algorithmic procedure.

We now present the BCR algorithm. We let
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{A}\to\Delta_{A}
be a Lipschitz-continuous function extending the identity function on
\Delta_{A}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an
R
-arity
(d,\tau)
-quasirandom
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust polymorphism. Pick
\eta>0
suitably small.

•

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a weighted instance of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
on variable set
X
.

•

Solve the basic SDP for
\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\CSP(\mathbf{A})
to get a unit vector
\mathbf{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and vectors
\mathbf{v}_{x,a}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
for all
x\in X
and
a\in A
with completeness at least
1-\alpha
(see
Section

6.2.2
).

•

Sample
p\sim\mathcal{P}
and independently sample
R
vectors
\zeta^{(1)},\ldots,\zeta^{(R)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0^{N},I_{N})
.

•

For all
x\in X
,
a\in A
, and
i\in[R]
, compute

g_{x,a,i}:=\langle\mathbf{v}_{x,a},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle+\langle\mathbf{v}_{x,a}-\mathbf{v}_{0}\langle\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{x,a}\rangle,\zeta^{(i)}\rangle.

•

For each
x\in X
and
i\in[R]
, compute

h_{x,i}:=\Psi(g_{x,a,i}:a\in A).

•

Let
H_{\eta}
be the multilinear polynomial corresponding to the function
T_{1-\eta}p:\Delta_{A}^{R}\to\Delta_{B}
.
17
17

17

Here, we interpret the map
p:A^{R}\to B
as real-valued function
p:A^{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{B}
where
p(\mathbf{a})_{b}=1
if
p(\mathbf{a})=b
and
0
otherwise. Then, we apply the noise operator
T_{1-\eta}
coordinate-wise. One can show for any
\mathbf{a}\in\Delta_{A}^{R}
, we have that
T_{1-\eta}p(\mathbf{a})\in\Delta_{B}
.

For each
x\in X,

q_{x}:=H_{\eta}(h_{x,1},\ldots,h_{x,R})\in\Delta_{B}.

•

For each
x\in X
, assign
\sigma(x)
according to the probability distribution
q_{x}
.

6.2
Configurations and Rounding Schemes

In order to describe our modifications to the BCR algorithm, we need to first abstract out the essential properties of the SDP rounding scheme.

6.2.1
Configurations

Given a unit vector
\mathbf{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we define
\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}(\mathbf{v}_{0})\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{A}
to be the set of all
A
-tuples of vectors
\{\mathbf{v}_{a}:a\in A\}
satisfying the following criteria:

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{v}_{a},\mathbf{v}_{a^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0\ \ \ \forall a\neq a^{\prime}\in A

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}\mathbf{v}_{a}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{0}.

We call these
A
-tuples
local configurations
and typically denote them by
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
. Note for each
a\in A
, there is an
integral
local configuration
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
for which
\mathbf{v}_{a}=\mathbf{v}_{0}
and
\mathbf{v}_{a^{\prime}}=0
for all
a^{\prime}\in A\setminus\{a\}
. We let
\mathbf{I}_{a}
denote this integral local configuration.

When
\mathbf{v}_{0}
is fixed in a context, for succinctness we let
\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
denote
\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}(\mathbf{v}_{0})
. We define a (normed) metric on
\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
by

\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{a\in A}\|\mathbf{v}_{a}-\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{a}\|^{2}_{2}}.

Note that, if
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
are interpreted as
matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{N\times A}
, the above is the Frobenius matrix norm.

We now define a
global configuration
to be a tuple of local configurations

\mathcal{V}:=(\mathbf{V}_{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}:x\in X)\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}.

In particular, given
\mathbf{a}\in A^{X}
, we let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{a}}:=(\mathbf{I}_{a_{x}}:x\in X)
denote the integral global configuration. Given
\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
, we let
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})\in\mathbb{R}^{(X\times A)^{2}}
denote the Gram matrix of all dot products. That is, for any
(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in X\times A
, we have that

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})_{(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})}:=\langle\mathbf{v}_{x,a},\mathbf{v}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle.

For
\mathbf{a}\in A^{X}
, we let
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})
be shorthand for
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{a}})
. In other words
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})_{(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})}=1
iff
\mathbf{a}_{x}=a
and
\mathbf{a}_{x^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}
, and
0
otherwise. Observe that
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})
can be identified with the Kronecker product
\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}\otimes\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}
, with
\Pi_{\mathbf{a}}
defined as in Section
5
.

It shall be useful to notice that, for any global configuration
\mathcal{V}
, the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\Tr(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}))=\sum_{(x,a)\in X\times A}\|\mathbf{v}_{x,a}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{x\in X}\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}=|X|.

(29)

6.2.2
SDP solutions and Rounding Schemes

Given a (weighted) instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
on variable set
X
, any valid solution to the SDP relaxation of
\mathcal{X}
can be viewed as a global configuration
\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
for some suitably large dimension
N
(which can be made at most
|X|\cdot|A|
, although we will often consider
N
to be larger).
However, not every
\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
is a valid solution to the SDP as each clause
(Y,P,Q)
of
\mathcal{X}
imposes some structure on
\mathcal{V}
. More precisely, if we let
\mathcal{V}|_{Y}:=(\mathbf{V}_{y}:y\in Y)
, then there exists a probability distribution
\Lambda_{Y,P}
on
A^{Y}
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}|_{Y})=\underset{\mathbf{a}\sim\Lambda_{Y,P}}{\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})].

(30)

We say that
\Lambda_{Y,P}
is
consistent
with
\mathcal{V}
if (
30
) holds. Note that this is the translation of the second-moment condition (
3
), while the first-moment condition (
2
) is implicit in the definition of local configurations.

We let
\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},A}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
be the set of all
\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
that satisfy (
30
) for some choice of
\Lambda_{Y,P}
for all constraints
(Y,P,Q)
of
\mathcal{X}
. We further define the map
\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}:\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}\to\mathbb{[}0,1]
to be

\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V}):=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\Lambda_{Y,P}\mid(Y,P,Q)\in\mathcal{X}\}\\
\text{ consistent with $\mathcal{V}$}\end{subarray}}\underset{(Y,P,Q)\sim\mathcal{X}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Pr_{\mathbf{a}\sim\Lambda_{Y,P}}[\mathbf{a}\in P]\right].

Analogously, we define
\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}:\Delta_{B}^{X}\to[0,1]
to be

\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{z}):=\underset{(Y,P,Q)\sim\mathcal{X}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in Q}\prod_{y\in Y}z_{y}(q_{y})\right].

Intuitively,
\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{z})
is the expected value of a solution obtained by independently assigning each
x\in X
a value from
B
according to the distribution
z_{x}
.
To maximize the soundness, we seek to have control on how these distributions
z_{x}\in\Delta_{B}
are selected. This motivates the notion of an (oblivious) rounding scheme.

Definition 6.5

(Rounding scheme)
.

Let
\Xi
be a probability distribution. We define an (oblivious)
rounding scheme
to be a function
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi\to\Delta_{B}
. If
S
can be computed efficiently, then we say that
S
is a
rounding algorithm
.

Definition 6.6

(Robust rounding scheme)
.

We say that a rounding scheme
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi\to\Delta_{B}
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust for an instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
if for all global configurations
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
, we have that

\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})\geq 1-\alpha\implies\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(S(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi):x\in X)]\geq 1-\beta.

Note that once
\xi\sim\Xi
is selected, each
x\in X
is rounded independently according to
S(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi)
. However, the randomness in
\Xi
will allow for nontrivial correlations which we systematically analyze. For the BCR algorithm, we consider the probability distribution
\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{N,\theta}:=\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{N}(0^{N},I_{N})^{R}
, where
\mathcal{P}
is the
R
-arity
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust, quasirandom polymorphism that gives a loss of
\theta
in
Theorem

6.4
. Then, we can think of the BCR algorithm as a rounding algorithm
\operatorname{BCR}^{N}_{\theta}:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{N,\theta}\to\Delta_{B}
for any choices of
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta>0
. In particular, once the SDP solution is found, the assigned probability distribution to each variable only depends on the global randomness chosen by
\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{N,\theta}
. We can then restate the second implication in
Theorem

6.4
as follows.

Corollary 6.7
.

Assume UGC. If
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust, then for any
\theta>0
and any instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, for all sufficiently large
N
(as a function of
\theta
and
|\mathcal{X}|
),
\operatorname{BCR}^{N}_{\theta}:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{N,\theta}\to\Delta_{B}
is
(\alpha-\theta,\beta+\theta)
-robust.

Using
Corollary

6.7
as the only property of the
\operatorname{BCR}
algorithm we need, we shall construct another rounding procedure meeting the requirements of
Theorem

6.1
.

6.3
Smoothing Configurations

We say that
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
are
\delta
-modifications
of each other for some
\delta\geq 0
if for all
x\in X
, we have that
\|\mathbf{V}_{x}-\mathbf{W}_{x}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\delta
. We let
B(\mathcal{V},\delta)\subseteq\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
denote the set of all
\delta
-modifications of
\mathcal{V}
. Note that if
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
are
\delta
-modifications with
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
, it may not be the case that
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
as there may be a clause
(Y,P,Q)
of
\mathcal{X}
for which no marginal distribution
\Lambda_{Y,P}
is consistent with
\mathcal{W}
.

As a key definition, let
\mathbb{V}^{N,\delta}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}:=\bigcup_{\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}}B(\mathcal{V},\delta)
. As an important step in constructing our rounding scheme, we first seek to extend
\operatorname{BCR}^{N}_{\theta}
from being robust for
\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
to being robust for
\mathbb{V}^{N,\delta}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
.

Definition 6.8

(Spaciously Robust rounding scheme)
.

Given an instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, we say that a rounding scheme
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi\to\Delta_{B}
is
(\delta,\alpha,\beta)
-spaciously robust for
\mathcal{X}
if for all global configurations
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
, and all
\mathcal{W}\in B(\mathcal{V},\delta)
,
we have that

\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})\geq 1-\alpha\implies\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(S(\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X)]\geq 1-\beta.

Clearly, for all weighted instances of the PCSP, any spaciously robust rounding scheme is in particular robust, as
\mathcal{V}\in B(\mathcal{V},\delta)
for any
\delta
.
Note also that in general
\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{W})
is not defined (since, as noted above, it may be that
\mathcal{W}\not\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
), so we use the completeness of
\mathcal{V}
as a proxy.

6.3.1
Smooth Lifting

Given an
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust scheme
S
, we would like a general method to turn it into a
(\delta,\alpha,\beta)
-spaciously robust scheme
S^{\prime}
. To that end, we use a padding trick similar to one used by Raghavendra and Steurer
[
RS09
]
.

Definition 6.9
.

Given
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and
\rho\in[0,1]
, we let
\mathbf{u}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{M+N}
be the
\rho
-concatenation of
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
; i.e., the vector defined by

(\mathbf{u}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v})_{i}:=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\rho}u_{i}&i\in[M]\\
\sqrt{1-\rho}v_{i}&i\in[M+N]\setminus[M].\end{cases}

for each
i\in[M+N]
.

Definition 6.10
.

Given
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{S}^{M}_{A}(\mathbf{u}_{0})
,
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}(\mathbf{v}_{0})
, and
\rho\in[0,1]
, we define the
\rho
-concatenation of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
as the list
\mathbf{U}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{V}:=(\mathbf{u}_{a}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{a}:a\in A)
.

Proposition 6.11
.

\mathbf{U}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{M+N}_{A}(\mathbf{u}_{0}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{0})
.

Proof.

First, for any distinct
a,a^{\prime}\in A
, we have that

\langle\mathbf{u}_{a}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{a},\mathbf{u}_{a^{\prime}}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{a^{\prime}}\rangle=\rho\langle\mathbf{u}_{a},\mathbf{u}_{a^{\prime}}\rangle+(1-\rho)\langle\mathbf{v}_{a},\mathbf{v}_{a^{\prime}}\rangle=0.

Second,

\sum_{a\in A}\mathbf{u}_{a}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{a}=\left(\sum_{a\in A}\mathbf{u}_{a}\right)\oplus_{\rho}\left(\sum_{a\in A}\mathbf{v}_{a}\right)=\mathbf{u}_{0}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{0}.\qed

Likewise, for
\mathcal{U}\in(\mathbb{S}^{M}_{A})^{X}
and
\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
, we can define
\mathcal{U}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V}:=(\mathbf{U}_{x}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{V}_{x}:x\in X)
. Recall the Gram matrix
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})
of a global configuration
\mathcal{V}
defined in
Section

6.2.1
.

Proposition 6.12
.

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V})=\rho\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U})+(1-\rho)\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}).

Proof.

For any
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V})_{(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{u}_{x,a}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{x,a},\mathbf{u}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{v}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle.

\displaystyle=\rho\langle\mathbf{u}_{x,a},\mathbf{u}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle+(1-\rho)\langle\mathbf{v}_{x,a},\mathbf{v}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\rho\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U})_{(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})}+(1-\rho)\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})_{(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})},

as desired.
∎

We now construct a specific global configuration
\mathcal{U}
that will be useful for our rounding scheme.

Definition 6.13
.

We say that
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\in(\mathbb{S}^{M}_{A})^{X}
is a
uniform
configuration if for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A
, we have that

\langle\mathbf{u}_{x,a},\mathbf{u}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{|A|}&x=x^{\prime}\text{ and }a=a^{\prime}\\
0&x=x^{\prime}\text{ and }a\neq a^{\prime}\\
\frac{1}{|A|^{2}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proposition 6.14
.

For
M=|X|\cdot|A|
,
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}
is well-defined. Further, for any instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\CSP(\mathbf{A})
, we have that
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\in\mathbb{V}^{M}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
.

Proof.

First, it is straightforward to verify that

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A})=\frac{1}{|A|^{|X|}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{X}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}).

Thus,
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A})
is PSD and thus has a realization in
\mathbb{R}^{|X|\cdot|A|}
. Now, for any clause
P
of
\mathcal{X}
on variable set
Y\subseteq X
, we can also verify that

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A})|_{(Y\times A)^{2}}=\frac{1}{|A|^{|Y|}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}).

Thus, for each clause of
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}
is consistent with a uniformly random assignment. Thus,
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\in\mathbb{V}^{M}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}.

∎

Given
\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{X}
and
\rho\in[0,1]
, we let
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{M+N}_{A})^{X}
be the
\rho
-lift
of
\mathcal{V}
. We next use
\rho
-lifting to make (
30
) more likely to be true.

6.3.2
Lifting
\delta
-modifications

Our current goal is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a weighted instance of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. For any
\delta>0
, there exists
\rho=O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta})\in[0,1]
such that for all
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathcal{W}\in B(\mathcal{V},\delta)
, we have that

(1)

\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{V}^{|X|\cdot|A|+N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
.

(2)

\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W})\geq\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta}).

To prove this lemma, we need some machinery from convex geometry. Some ideas are inspired by Raghavendra–Steurer
[
RS09
]
and Appendix A of
[
BGS25
]
. We start with a simple proposition.

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be vectors of length at most
1
, such that
\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\delta
and
\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\delta
. Then
|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\leq 2\sqrt{\delta}
.

Proof.

Using Cauchy–Schwarz, notice that

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|+|\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\delta}.\qed

Next, we fix a finite (nonempty) set
Y
. We let
K_{Y,A},K^{0}_{Y,A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{(Y\times A)^{2}}
denote the following two convex sets, that are linear subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{(Y\times A)^{2}}
:

\displaystyle K_{Y,A}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}):\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{R}\right\},

\displaystyle K^{0}_{Y,A}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}):\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}=0\right\}.

We will need the following key lemma. It can be directly derived from a combinatorial characterisation of low-dimensional projections of a hypermatrix proved in
[
CŽ23a
]
. Moreover, we also give a self-contained proof involving the dual space
K_{Y,A}^{*}
of
K_{Y,A}
. Full details of both arguments are postponed to
Appendix

A
.

Lemma 6.17
.

For any
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}\in(\mathbb{S}_{A}^{N})^{Y}
, we have that

(1)

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})\in K_{Y,A}
.

(2)

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})-\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W})\in K_{Y,A}^{0}
.

We just need a bit more machinery, then we can prove
Lemma

6.15
. Define the set

L_{Y,A}:=\{M\in K^{0}_{Y,A}:\|M\|_{\infty}\leq 1\},

where
\|M\|_{\infty}
is the absolute value of the greatest entry of
M
. Note that
L_{Y,A}
is convex and compact. Define also the function
f:K^{0}_{Y,A}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

f(M):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\sum_{\mathbf{a}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}=0\\
\sum_{\mathbf{a}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})=M\end{subarray}}\max_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}|\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}|.

We now prove the following.

Proposition 6.18
.

\kappa_{Y,A}:=\sup_{M\in L_{Y,A}}f(M)
is finite.

Proof.

Pick an arbitrary basis
M_{1},\ldots,M_{r}
of
K^{0}_{Y,A}
. It is straightforward to see that
f(M_{i})<\infty
for all
i\in[r]
. Furthermore, if
M=\beta_{1}M_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{r}M_{r}
, then

\displaystyle f(M)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}|\beta_{i}|f(M_{i}).

(31)

Let
g(M)
be the RHS of (
31
). Since
M_{1},\ldots,M_{r}
form a basis, the map
M\mapsto(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r})
is continuous. Thus,
g(M)
is continuous. Thus, since
L
is compact,
\max_{M\in L_{Y,A}}g(M)
exists and is finite. Thus,
\kappa_{Y,A}:=\sup_{M\in L_{Y,A}}f(M)
is finite as well.
∎

We also need the following fact.

Proposition 6.19
.

We have that
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{|X|\cdot|A|+N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V})\geq\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\rho).

(32)

Proof.

Fix a clause
(Y,P)
of
\mathcal{X}
with
Y\subseteq X
and
P\in\mathbf{A}
. Pick a probability distribution
(p_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y})
such that
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}|_{Y})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}p_{\mathbf{a}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})
and
\operatorname{comp}_{Y,P}(\mathcal{V}|_{Y})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in P}p_{\mathbf{a}}
. By methods similar to that of
Proposition

6.14
, we can observe that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}((\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V})|_{Y})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\left(\frac{\rho}{|A|^{|Y|}}+(1-\rho)p_{\mathbf{a}}\right)\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}).

(33)

Thus,
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{|X|\cdot|A|+N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{comp}_{Y,P}((\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V})|_{Y})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in P}\left(\frac{\rho}{|A|^{|Y|}}+(1-\rho)p_{\mathbf{a}}\right)\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle\geq(1-\rho)\operatorname{comp}_{Y,P}(\mathcal{V}|_{Y})

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{comp}_{Y,P}(\mathcal{V}|_{Y})-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\rho),

where we use that the completeness is at most
1
. Taking the average of the above inequality for all clauses of
\mathcal{X}
, we get (
32
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.15
.

Keep
\rho\in[0,1]
arbitrary for the moment. Fix a clause
(Y,P,Q)
of
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V})|_{Y}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W})|_{Y}
. To prove (1) it suffices to show that
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
has a solution to (
30
). Recall from
Proposition

6.19
and (
33
) that
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{V}^{|X|\cdot|A|+N}_{Y,P}
and

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\left(\frac{\rho}{|A|^{|Y|}}+(1-\rho)p_{\mathbf{a}}\right)\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}).

Since
\mathcal{W}\in B(\mathcal{V},\delta)
, we have that
\|(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{V})-(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W})\|_{2}^{2}\leq(1-\rho)\delta
. By
Proposition

6.16
, we have that
