\|\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})-\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\rho)\delta}.

That is,
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{(1-\rho)\delta}}(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})-\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}))\in L_{Y,A}
. Thus, by
Lemma

6.17
and
Proposition

6.18
, there exists a choice of
\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{R}
for all
\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}
such that
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})-\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a})
and
|\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}|\leq 2\kappa_{Y,A}\sqrt{(1-\rho)\delta}
. Pick
\rho
minimal such that

2\kappa_{Y,A}\sqrt{(1-\rho)\delta}\leq\frac{\rho}{|A|^{|Y|}}

for every clause
(Y,P,Q)
of
\mathcal{X}
. Note that
\rho=O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta})
and
\rho\in[0,1]
because the LHS is
0
when
\rho=1
. As such, we now have that

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\left(\frac{\rho}{|A|^{|Y|}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}+(1-\rho)p_{\mathbf{a}}\right)\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}),

where every coefficient is nonzero. Thus,
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
satisfies (
30
), so
\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{V}^{|X|\cdot|A|+N}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}}
. This proves (1).

To prove (2), note that for a fixed clause
(Y,P,Q)
of
\mathcal{X}
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{comp}_{Y,P}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in P}\left(\frac{\rho}{|A|^{|Y|}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}+(1-\rho)p_{\mathbf{a}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\rho)\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in P}p_{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle=(1-\rho)\operatorname{comp}_{Y,P}(\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{comp}_{Y,P}(\mathcal{V})-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta}).

Averaging this inequality over all clauses
(Y,P,Q)
of
\mathcal{X}
proves (2), as desired.
∎

6.3.3
The Pullback Scheme

In general, for an SDP rounding scheme to be robust, we need that SDP vectors which are close to each other have similar soundness when rounded.
A priori
, we do not know if the
\operatorname{BCR}
rounding schemes have such a property. Toward, we show how to smooth the
\operatorname{BCR}
rounding scheme using a technique we call the “Pullback Scheme.” In
Section

6.5
we will further modify the pullback scheme to have even stronger robustness properties.

For a fixed
\theta>0
and a fixed instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, by
Corollary

6.7
there exists
N
such that
\operatorname{BCR}^{N^{\prime}}_{\theta}
is an
(\alpha-\beta,\beta+\theta)
-robust scheme for
\mathcal{X}
for all
N^{\prime}\geq N
. In particular, for any finite set
Z
, we have that
\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}
is an
(\alpha-\theta,\beta+\theta)
-robust scheme for
\mathcal{X}
. We now define a
pullback scheme

\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\rho}
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
as follows.

Definition 6.20

(Pullback Scheme)
.

For any finite set
Z
, we define
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}\to\Delta_{B}
to be

\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V},\xi)=\underset{z\sim Z}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}(\mathbf{U}_{z}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{V},\xi)\right],

where
z\sim Z
is the uniform distribution,
\mathbf{U}_{z}
is the
z
-th local configuration of
\mathcal{U}_{Z,A}
, and
\rho=O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta})
is chosen according to
Lemma

6.15
.

Note that we call this a “pullback” scheme as we are mapping a higher-dimensional rounding scheme to a lower dimension. We now show this pullback is spaciously robust.

Lemma 6.21
.

If
\operatorname{BCR}^{|X|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}
is an
(\alpha-\theta,\beta+\theta)
-robust scheme for an instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, then
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
is a
(\delta,\alpha^{\prime},\beta+\theta+\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|})
-spaciously robust scheme for
\mathcal{X}
, where
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha-\theta-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
satisfy
\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})\geq 1-\alpha^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{W}\in B(\mathcal{V},\delta)
. By
Definition

6.20
and the fact that
\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}
is multilinear, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X)]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}\left(\underset{z\sim Z}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}(\mathbf{U}_{z}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi)\right]:x\in X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\\
{\mathbf{z}\sim Z^{X}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}\left(\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}(\mathbf{U}_{z_{x}}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X\right)\right],

where
\mathbf{z}\sim Z^{X}
is chosen uniformly at random. For each
\mathbf{z}\in Z^{X}
, let
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}}:=(\mathbf{U}_{z_{x}}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{W}_{x}:x\in X)
. If
\mathbf{z}
is injective—that is, if
z_{x}=z_{x^{\prime}}
implies
x=x^{\prime}
—then we claim that

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}})=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W}).

To see why, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}})_{(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\rho\langle\mathbf{U}_{z_{x},a},\mathbf{U}_{z_{x^{\prime}},a^{\prime}}\rangle+(1-\rho)\langle\mathbf{W}_{x,a},\mathbf{U}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\rho\langle\mathbf{U}_{x,a},\mathbf{U}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle+(1-\rho)\langle\mathbf{W}_{x,a},\mathbf{U}_{x^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle

(
z
injective)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{U}_{X,A}\oplus_{\rho}\mathcal{W})_{(x,a),(x^{\prime},a^{\prime})}.

Thus, by
Lemma

6.15
, for all injective
\mathbf{z}
, we have that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{V}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
and

\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{z}})\geq\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta})=1-\alpha+\theta.

Thus, since
\operatorname{BCR}^{|X|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}
is
(\alpha-\theta,\beta+\theta)
-robust, we have that

\displaystyle 1-\beta-\theta

\displaystyle\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\\
{\mathbf{z}\sim Z^{X}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}\left(\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}(\mathbf{U}_{z_{x}}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X\right)\middle|\text{ $\mathbf{z}$ injective}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Pr_{\mathbf{z}\sim Z^{X}}[\mathbf{z}\text{ injective}]}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\\
{\mathbf{z}\sim Z^{X}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}\left(\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}(\mathbf{U}_{z_{x}}\oplus_{\rho}\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Pr_{\mathbf{z}\sim Z^{X}}[\mathbf{z}\text{ injective}]}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X)].

To finish, we note that

\Pr_{\mathbf{z}\sim Z^{X}}[\mathbf{z}\text{ injective}]=\prod_{i=1}^{|X|}\left(1-\frac{i-1}{|Z|}\right)\geq 1-\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|},

so

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X)]\geq(1-\beta-\theta)\cdot(1-\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|})\geq 1-\beta-\theta-\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|},

as desired.
∎

6.4
Measure Theory Fundamentals

Our next step toward the proof of
Theorem

6.1
is to modify
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}
to another scheme (which we will call
\operatorname{REQ}
) so that similar inputs give similar outputs. In order to do this, we first need some fundamentals of measure theory. We refer to the textbook of DiBenedetto
[
DiB16
]
for foundational concepts.

Let
R
be a possibly infinite set, and let
\mathcal{R}:=(R,\mu)
be a probability measure over
R
. See Chapter 3 of
[
DiB16
]
for a precise definition.
Recall that a function
f:R\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
measurable
if the preimage under
f
of every measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is measurable.
Let also
\int_{R}
denote the Lebesgue integral over
R
.
We shall consider the set
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
consisting of the quotient space of the set of all measurable functions
f:R\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

such that
\int_{R}\|f(x)\|_{2}^{2}\,d\mu(x)<\infty
,
modulo the equivalence relation “
\sim
” defined by
f\sim g
if and only if
f-g
is only nonzero on a set of zero measure.
Observe that
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a normed space, with the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{2,2}=\sqrt{\int_{R}\|f(x)\|_{2}^{2}\,d\mu(x)}.

(34)

We say that
f\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a limit of a sequence of functions
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
if, for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|f-f_{i}\|_{2,2}\leq\epsilon
for all
i\geq N
. It is clear that every sequence has at most one limit. A
Cauchy sequence
is a sequence of functions
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that, for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|f_{i}-f_{j}\|_{2,2}\leq\epsilon
for any
i,j\geq N
. We say that
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a
Banach space
if every Cauchy sequence has a limit. The following is a simple consequence of the Riesz–Fischer theorem.

Theorem 6.22

(Riesz–Fischer, see Chapter 6, Theorem 5.1 of
[
DiB16
]
)
.

L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R})=L^{2}(\mathcal{R})
is a Banach space.
18
18

18

Technically, the treatment in
[
DiB16
]
allows for
f
to take on the value
\pm\infty
, but it is clear from (
34
) that
f^{-1}(\{-\infty,\infty\})
must have zero measure in order for
\|f\|_{2,2}<\infty
. Thus, we can assume
f
has finite output.

Corollary 6.23
.

For all
d\geq 1
,
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Banach space.

Proof.

Consider any Cauchy sequence
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For each
i\in[d]
, note that
f_{1,i},f_{2,i},\ldots
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R})
. Thus, by
Theorem

6.22
, this sequence converges to
g_{i}\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R})
. Let
g=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{d})
. It is easy to check that
g\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore, since
d
is finite, we have that

\displaystyle 0=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lim_{i\to\infty}\|f_{i,j}-g_{j}\|^{2}_{2,2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|f_{i,j}-g_{j}\|^{2}_{2,2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}\|f_{i}-g\|_{2,2}.

Thus,
g
is indeed the limit of the Cauchy sequence
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots
, as desired.
∎

We say that
K\subseteq L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
convex
if, for any
f_{1},f_{2}\in K
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
, we have that
\lambda f_{1}+(1-\lambda)f_{2}\in K
. We let
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\Delta_{d})\subseteq L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the subset of functions whose range lies in
\Delta_{d}
. Note that
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\Delta_{d})
is convex.

Given
K\subseteq L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we let
\overline{K}\subseteq L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote its (sequential)
closure
. That is,
f\in\overline{K}
if and only if, for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
g\in K
with
\|f-g\|_{2,2}\leq\epsilon
. Clearly if
K
is convex then so is
\overline{K}
.
The following fact is well known. To get some familiarity with the definitions, we include a simple proof.

Proposition 6.24
.

If
K\subseteq L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\Delta_{d})
, then
\overline{K}\subseteq L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\Delta_{d})
.

Proof.

Assume for sake of contradiction that there is
f\in\overline{K}
with
f\not\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\Delta_{d})
. Let
S=f^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\Delta_{d})
. Note that
\mu(S)>0
. Since
f
is measurable, there must exist
\delta>0
and
T\subseteq S
such that
\mu(T)>0
and, for all
x\in T
, the
\ell_{1}
distance between
x
and
\Delta_{d}
is greater than
d
. Therefore, for all
g\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\Delta_{d})
, we have that

\|f-g\|_{2,2}^{2}\geq\int_{T}\|f(x)-g(x)\|_{1}^{2}\,d\mu(x)\geq\delta^{2}\mu(T).

Since
\delta^{2}\mu(T)>0
, this contradicts the fact that
f\in\overline{K}
.
∎

We next observe an elementary fact about our norm for
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proposition 6.25
.

If
f,g\in L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy
\|f-g\|_{2,2}\geq\delta
then

\|(f+g)/2\|^{2}_{2,2}\leq\max(\|f\|^{2}_{2,2},\|g\|^{2}_{2,2})-\delta^{2}/4.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\|(f+g)/2\|_{2,2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{R}\|f(x)+g(x)\|_{2}^{2}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{R}2\|f(x)\|_{2}^{2}+2\|g(x)\|_{2}^{2}-\|f(x)-g(x)\|_{2}^{2}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(2\|f\|_{2,2}^{2}+2\|g\|_{2,2}^{2}-\|f-g\|_{2,2}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\max(\|f\|^{2}_{2,2},\|g\|^{2}_{2,2})-\delta^{2}/4.\qed

We now show that we can uniquely solve a suitable optimization problem over
L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\Delta_{d})
.

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
K\subseteq L^{2,2}(\mathcal{R},\mathbb{R}^{d})
be nonempty and convex, then there exists a unique
f\in\overline{K}
for which
\|f\|_{2,2}
is minimized.

Proof.

First, note that if a minimizer exists it must be unique. Otherwise, there are
f,g\in\overline{K}
for which
\|f\|_{2,2}=\|g\|_{2,2}
is the minimum, but
Proposition

6.25
shows that
(f+g)/2\in\overline{K}
must have a smaller norm for
\|f-g\|_{2,2}>0
by definition of being distinct (recall that for
f
and
g
to be distinct we require that
f\neq g
on a set of nonzero measure).

Thus, it suffices to prove that a minimizer exists. Let
\alpha=\inf\{\|f\|_{2,2}^{2}:f\in\overline{K}\}<\infty
. By definition of the infimum, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
f_{n}\in\overline{K}
for which
\|f_{n}\|_{2,2}^{2}\leq\alpha+\frac{1}{n}
. Now consider any positive integers
n\geq m
and observe by
Proposition

6.25
that

\alpha\leq\|(f_{n}+f_{m})/2\|^{2}_{2,2}\leq\max(\|f_{n}\|^{2}_{2,2},\|f_{m}\|^{2}_{2,2})-\frac{1}{4}\|f_{n}-f_{m}\|^{2}_{2,2}\leq\alpha+\frac{1}{m}-\frac{1}{4}\|f_{n}-f_{m}\|^{2}_{2,2}.

Thus,
\|f_{n}-f_{m}\|^{2}_{2,2}\leq 4/m
. Hence
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots
is a Cauchy sequence. By
Theorem

6.22
, we have that its limit
f
lives in
\overline{K}
. Furthermore, since
\|f_{m}\|^{2}_{2,2}
approaches
\alpha
as
m\to\infty
, it is straightforward to show that
\|f\|^{2}_{2,2}=\alpha
. Thus,
f
is the unique minimizer of the norm in
\overline{K}
.
∎

6.5
Defining
\operatorname{REQ}

Recall that in
Section

6.3.3
, we defined a family of spaciously robust rounding schemes
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}
. We now seek to further modify
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}
into another scheme called
\operatorname{REQ}
which supports an additional property: any two close vectors round to essentially the same distribution over
\Delta_{B}
. We achieve this via convex optimization.

Recall we previously defined a
rounding scheme
to be a function
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi\to\Delta_{B}
(
Definition

6.5
). Equivalently, we can think of
S
as having signature
\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\to L^{2,2}(\Xi,\Delta_{B})
. We assume this latter presentation from now on.

Definition 6.27

(
\delta
-spread)
.

Recall that, for any
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
,
B(\mathbf{V},\delta)
denotes the set of all
\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
with
\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\delta
. We define a
\delta
-spread
of
\mathbf{V}
to be a finitely supported
probability distribution
\Lambda
over
B(\mathbf{V},\delta)
such that for all
p\in[0,1]
, we have that

\Pr_{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda}[\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta]\geq p.

We say that
S^{\prime}:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\to L^{2,2}(\Xi,\Delta_{B})
is a
\delta
-smoothing
of
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\to L^{2,2}(\Xi,\Delta_{B})
if, for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
, there exists a
\delta
-spread
\Lambda_{\mathbf{V}}
of
\mathbf{V}
such that

S^{\prime}(\mathbf{V})=\underset{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda_{\mathbf{V}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{W})\right].

We let
B(S,\mathbf{V},\delta)\subseteq L^{2,2}(\Xi,\Delta_{B})
denote all possible values of
S^{\prime}(\mathbf{V})
.

Proposition 6.28
.

B(S,\mathbf{V},\delta)
is convex.

Proof.

Consider any
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\in B(S,\mathbf{V},\delta)
and let
\Lambda^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
be
\delta
-spreads of
\mathbf{V}
such that
S^{\prime}(\mathbf{V})=\underset{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{W})\right]
and
S^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{V})=\underset{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{W})\right]
. For any
\theta\in[0,1]
, let
\Lambda_{\theta}
be the probability distribution that samples from
\Lambda^{\prime}
with probability
\theta
and from
\Lambda^{\prime\prime}
with probability
1-\theta
. It is not hard to see that

(\theta S^{\prime}+(1-\theta)S^{\prime\prime})(\mathbf{V})=\underset{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda_{\theta}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{W})\right].

It remains to verify that
\Lambda_{\theta}
is a
\delta
-spread of
\mathbf{V}
. To see why, note that for any
p\in[0,1]
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda_{\theta}}[\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta]

\displaystyle=\theta\Pr_{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda^{\prime}}[\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta]+(1-\theta)\Pr_{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda^{\prime\prime}}[\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta]

\displaystyle\geq\theta p+(1-\theta)p=p

as desired.
∎

We now define
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\to L^{2,2}(\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta},\Delta_{B})
in terms of
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\to L^{2,2}(\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta},\Delta_{B})
as follows.

Definition 6.29
.

For all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
and sets
Z
, we let

\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V}):=\operatorname{argmin}\{\|f\|_{2,2}:f\in\overline{B(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta},\mathbf{V},\delta)}\}.

Note that the scheme is well defined by
Lemma

6.26
. Intuitively, the argmin performs a local optimization over many
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}
rounding schemes. This minimum is fairly robust to modification of the inputs. In particular, we have the following two lemmas which are proved in the next two subsections. The first lemma says that the robustness of
\operatorname{REQ}
for
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
barely degrades in comparison to
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}
. The second lemma says that
\operatorname{REQ}
rounds nearby vectors to similar distributions.

Lemma 6.30
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a weighted instance of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. Assume
\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}
is
(\alpha-\theta,\beta+\theta)
-robust for
\mathcal{X}
. Then
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
is
(\alpha-\theta-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta}),\beta+\theta+\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|})
-robust for
\mathcal{X}
, where
O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta})
is from
Lemma

6.21
.

Lemma 6.31
.

Assume
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
with
\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\delta\cdot\eta^{2}
for some
\eta>0
. Then,
\|\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V})-\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W})\|_{2,2}\leq 8\sqrt{\eta}
.

6.5.1
Robustness is Preserved:
Lemma

6.30

Proving
Lemma

6.30
is relatively straightforward. Since,
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
is already spaciously robust, it suffices to show that any
\delta
-spread which
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
could select has similar soundness.

Proof of
Lemma

6.30
.

By
Lemma

6.26
, the definition of
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
is well-defined. Thus, for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists a
\delta
-smoothing
S
of
\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
such that for all
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
, we have that

\|\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V})-S(\mathbf{V})\|_{2,2}=\sqrt{\underset{\xi\in\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}{\mathbb{E}}[(\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V},\xi)-S(\mathbf{V},\xi))^{2}]}<\epsilon.

Let
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha-\theta-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta})
. Fix
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
for which
\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})\geq 1-\alpha^{\prime}
. For each
x\in X
, let
\Lambda_{x}
be a probability distribution over
B(\mathbf{V}_{x},\delta)
such that

\displaystyle S(\mathbf{V})=\underset{\mathbf{W}\sim\Lambda_{x}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W})\right].

(35)

Let
\mathbb{W}
be a finite probability distribution over
B(\mathcal{V},\delta)
, where for each
x\in X
, the distribution
\mathbf{W}_{x}
(with
\mathcal{W}\sim\mathbb{W}
) is an independent sample from
\Lambda_{x}
. Note that every
\mathcal{W}\in\supp\mathbb{W}
is a
\delta
-modification of
\mathcal{V}
, thus by
Lemma

6.21
we have that

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}W\sim\mathbb{W}\\
{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W},\xi):x\in X)\right]\geq 1-\beta-\theta-\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|}.

Like in the proof of
Lemma

6.21
, we use the fact that
\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}:\Delta_{B}^{X}\to[0,1]
is a multilinear function. Thus, since
\mathbb{W}
is a product distribution, we have that

\displaystyle 1-\beta-\theta-\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|}

\displaystyle\leq\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{W}_{x}\sim\Lambda_{x}}[\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi)]:x\in X)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(S(\mathbf{W}_{x},\xi):x\in X)\right],

where the last equality uses (
35
). Since
\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}:\Delta_{B}^{X}\to[0,1]
is bounded and multilinear, we have for any
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\Delta_{B}^{X}
the coarse bound

\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{p})-\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{q})\leq\sum_{x\in X}|p_{x}-q_{x}|\leq\sqrt{|X|\sum_{x\in X}(p_{x}-q_{x})^{2}},

where the latter inequality follows from Cauchy–Schwarz. Thus, since
\sqrt{\cdot}
is a concave function,

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(S(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi):x\in X)-\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi):x\in X)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{|X|}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\sum_{x\in X}(S(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi)-\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi))^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{|X|\cdot\sum_{x\in X}\underset{\xi\in\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}{\mathbb{E}}[(S(\mathbf{V},\xi)-\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V},\xi))^{2}]}

\displaystyle<\sqrt{|X|\cdot\sum_{x\in X}\epsilon^{2}}=|X|\epsilon.

Therefore,

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(\operatorname{REQ}^{N}_{\theta,\delta,x}(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi):x\in X)\right]\geq 1-\beta-\theta-|X|\epsilon.

Taking the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
proves that
\operatorname{REQ}^{N}_{\theta,\delta}
is
(\alpha-\theta-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta}),\beta+\theta)
-robust for
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

6.5.2
Smoothness of
\operatorname{REQ}^{N}_{\theta,\delta}
:
Lemma

6.31

We now prove
Lemma

6.31
. Intuitively, we seek to argue that for two nearby vectors
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
, the optimization in
Definition

6.29
gives similar answers. We do this by a “strategy stealing” argument, for any
\delta
-spread for
\mathbf{V}
, there is a similar
\delta
-spread for
\mathbf{W}
. This is done by taking the
\delta
-spread for
\mathbf{V}
and modifying it as little as possible to be a legal
\delta
-spread for
\mathbf{W}
. This leads to the following proposition.

Proposition 6.32
.

Let
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi\to\Delta_{B}
be a rounding scheme. Consider
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
with
\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\delta\cdot\eta^{2}
for
\eta\in(0,1)
. For any
f\in B(S,\mathbf{V},\delta)
, there is
g\in B(S,\mathbf{W},\delta)
with
\|f-g\|_{2,2}\leq 2\eta
.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
be a
\delta
-spread of
\mathbf{V}
such that

\displaystyle\forall p\in[0,1],\;p

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda}[\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta]

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})\right].

Recall that
\Lambda
is a discrete probability distribution. Let
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{k}\in B(\mathbf{V},\delta)
be the support of this distribution with
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in[0,1]
the corresponding probabilities. We assume that
\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\cdots\leq\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
. Pick
\ell\in[k]
minimal such that
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{\ell}\geq 1-\eta
. Let
\Lambda^{\prime}
be a probability distribution supported on
\{\mathbf{W},\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{\ell}\}
which samples
\mathbf{W}
with probability
\eta
,
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in[\ell-1]
with probability
p_{i}
, and samples
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{\ell}
otherwise (with probability
p^{\prime}_{\ell}:=1-\eta-p_{1}-\cdots-p_{\ell-1}
). We use the fact that

\displaystyle(p_{\ell}-p^{\prime}_{\ell})+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}p_{i}\leq\eta.

(36)

Informally,
\Lambda^{\prime}
samples
\mathbf{W}
with probability
\eta
and otherwise samples randomly from the
1-\eta
fraction of
\Lambda
that is closest to
\mathbf{V}
.

We next justify why
\Lambda^{\prime}
is a
\delta
-spread of
\mathbf{W}
. First note that if
\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta
then

\|\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\leq(\|\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{V}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|_{2})^{2}\leq(\eta\sqrt{\delta}+p\sqrt{\delta})^{2}=(p+\eta)^{2}\delta.

As such, for any
p\in[0,1]
, we have that

\Pr_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda}\left[\|\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta\right]\geq\Pr_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda}\left[\|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\max(p-\eta,0)^{2}\delta\right]\geq\max(p-\eta,0).

Therefore,

\Pr_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda^{\prime}}\left[\|\mathbf{W}-\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2}\leq p^{2}\delta\right]\geq\max(p-\eta,0)+\eta\geq p.

Thus,
\Lambda^{\prime}
is a
\delta
-spread of
\mathbf{W}
and thus supported in
B(\mathbf{W},\delta)
. Let
g=\underset{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})\right].
We have that

\displaystyle\|f-g\|_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\underset{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})\right]-\underset{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\sim\Lambda^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})\right]\right\|_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{i\in[k]}p_{i}S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i})-\eta S(\mathbf{W})-\sum_{i\in[\ell-1]}p_{i}S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i})-p^{\prime}_{\ell}S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{\ell})\right\|_{2,2}

\displaystyle\leq(p_{\ell}-p^{\prime}_{\ell})\|S(\mathbf{V}_{\ell})\|_{2,2}+\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}p_{i}\|S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i})\|_{2,2}+\eta\|S(\mathbf{W})\|_{2,2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta\max_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\in B(\mathbf{V},\delta)\cup B(\mathbf{W},\delta)}\|S(\mathbf{V}^{\prime})\|_{2,2}

(by (
36
))

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta,

as desired. ∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.31
.

If
\eta\geq 1
, then
\|\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V})-\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W})\|_{2,2}\leq\sqrt{2}<8\sqrt{\eta}
, as desired. Thus, assume
\eta\in(0,1)
.

Fix
x\in X
and let
f=\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V})
and
g=\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{W})
. By
Proposition

6.32
, we have that there is
f^{\prime}\in B(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta},\mathbf{V},\delta)
and
g^{\prime}\in B(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta},\mathbf{W},\delta)
with
\|f^{\prime}-g\|_{2,2}\leq 2\eta
and
\|g^{\prime}-f\|_{2,2}\leq 2\eta
. Let
g^{\prime\prime}=(g+g^{\prime})/2
and
f^{\prime\prime}=(f+f^{\prime})/2
. Assume WLOG that
\|f\|_{2,2}\geq\|g\|_{2,2}
. By
Proposition

6.25
and the fact that
\|f\|_{2,2}\leq 1
, we have that

\displaystyle\|f^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}_{2,2}

\displaystyle\leq\max(\|f\|^{2}_{2,2},\|f^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2,2})-\frac{\|f-f^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2,2}}{4}

\displaystyle\leq\max(\|f\|^{2}_{2,2},(\|g\|_{2,2}+2\eta)^{2})-\frac{(\|f-g\|_{2,2}-2\eta)^{2}}{4}

\displaystyle\leq(\|f\|_{2,2}+2\eta)^{2}-\frac{(\|f-g\|_{2,2}-2\eta)^{2}}{4}

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|^{2}_{2,2}+8\eta-\frac{(\|f-g\|_{2,2}-2\eta)^{2}}{4}.

Since
f^{\prime\prime}\in B(\widehat{\operatorname{BCR}}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta},\mathbf{V},\delta)
and
f=\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V})
, we have that
\|f^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}_{2,2}\geq\|f\|^{2}_{2,2}
. Thus,

8\eta\geq\frac{(\|f-g\|_{2,2}-2\eta)^{2}}{4}.

Thus,
\|f-g\|_{2,2}\leq\sqrt{32\eta}+2\eta\leq 8\sqrt{\eta}
, as desired.
∎

6.6
Correlated Rounding

The final necessary ingredient to prove
Theorem

6.1
is to convert the
independent
rounding of BCR into a
correlated
rounding. More precisely, by
Lemma

6.31
, we know that
\operatorname{REQ}
maps similar vectors to similar probability distributions. However, the final step of independent rounding in the BCR algorithm breaks this property. For example, for
A=\{0,1\}
, consider the points
p_{x}=(0.5,0.5)
and
p_{y}=(0.51,0.49)
in
\Delta_{A}
. The two distributions are highly similar, but with independent rounding there is a
1/2
chance that
x
and
y
are rounded differently. We can make these distributions correlated by sampling a common random real number
r\sim[0,1]
and then rounding
x
according to whether
r<0.5
and
y
according to whether
r<0.51
. Now, there is a
99\%
chance
x
and
y
are rounded to the same value.

We can perform analogous correlated rounding in general (with
\xi_{\operatorname{COR}}
corresponding the variable
r
). Let
\operatorname{plur}:\mathbb{R}^{B}\to B
be any function with the property that for all
q\in\Delta_{B}
,
q(\operatorname{plur}(q))\geq q(b)
for all
b\in B
. That is,
\operatorname{plur}
is a plurality function with ties broken arbitrarily (cf.
Section

5
).

Definition 6.33
.

Let
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\to L^{2,2}(\Xi,\Delta_{B})
be a rounding scheme. Let
\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B}
be the uniform distribution over
[0,\frac{1}{2|B|}]^{B}
. We then define
\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{COR}}:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\to L^{2,2}(\Xi\times\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B},\Delta_{B})
as follows:

\displaystyle S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V},\xi,\xi_{\operatorname{COR}})=\operatorname{plur}(S(\mathbf{V},\xi)+\xi_{\operatorname{COR}}).

(37)

Note that
S_{\operatorname{COR}}
only outputs elements of
B
, which we identify with the vertices of
\Delta_{B}
.

We seek to prove two facts about (
37
): First, if
S
is robust, then
S_{\operatorname{COR}}
is robust; second, if
\|S(\mathbf{V})-S(\mathbf{W})\|_{2,2}
is small, then
S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V})
and
S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{W})
have mostly equal outputs.

Lemma 6.34
.

Assume
S:\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}\times\Xi\to\Delta_{B}
is a rounding scheme that is
(\alpha,\beta)
-robust for a weighted instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. Then,
S_{\operatorname{COR}}
is
(\alpha,O_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta))
-robust for
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}
be an SDP solution with
\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})\geq 1-\alpha
. By
Definition

6.6
, we have that

\displaystyle\underset{\xi\sim\Xi}{\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(S(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi):x\in X)]\geq 1-\beta.

(38)

Let
(Y,P,Q)
be an arbitrary clause of
\mathcal{X}
and fix
\xi\in\supp\Xi
. Assume that
\operatorname{sound}_{Y,Q}(S(\mathbf{V}_{y},\xi):y\in Y)\geq 1-\gamma
. We claim that

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{p}\sim[0,1/(2|B|)]^{B}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\operatorname{sound}_{Y,Q}(S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V}_{y},\xi,\mathbf{p}):y\in Y)\right]\geq 1-(2|B|)^{|Y|}\gamma.

(39)

For notational convenience, we let
\mathbf{r}:=(S(\mathbf{V}_{y},\xi):y\in Y)\in\Delta_{B}^{Y}
. The assumption
\operatorname{sound}_{Y,Q}(\mathbf{q})\geq 1-\gamma
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in B^{Y}\setminus Q}\prod_{y\in Y}r_{y,q_{y}}\leq\gamma.

(40)

and we seek to prove that

\Pr_{\mathbf{p}\sim[0,1/(2|B|)]^{B}}[(\operatorname{plur}(\mathbf{r}_{y}+\mathbf{p}):y\in Y)\in Q]\geq 1-(2|B|)^{|Y|}\gamma.

Consider any
\mathbf{q}\in B^{Y}\setminus Q
for which there exists
\mathbf{p}\in[0,1/(2|B|)]^{B}
for which
(\operatorname{plur}(\mathbf{r}_{y}+\mathbf{p}):y\in Y)=\mathbf{q}
. If no such
\mathbf{q}
exists, then the LHS of (
38
) equals
1
.

For each
y\in Y
, in order for
\operatorname{plur}(\mathbf{r}_{y}+\mathbf{p})=q_{y}
, we must have that
r_{y,q_{y}}+\frac{1}{2|B|}\geq r_{y,b}
for all
b\in B
. Since
\sum_{b\in B}r_{y,b}\geq 1
, this means that
r_{y,q_{y}}\geq\frac{1}{2|B|}.
Thus, by (
40
), we have that

\gamma\geq\prod_{y\in Y}r_{y,q_{y}}\geq\frac{1}{(2|B|)^{|Y|}}.

Therefore, the RHS of (
39
) is at most zero, so the inequality is true. To finish, we apply (
39
) and (
38
) as follows.

\displaystyle\underset{\xi\sim\Xi}{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle[\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi):x\in X)]

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\sim\Xi\\
(Y,P,Q)\sim\mathcal{X}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[\operatorname{sound}_{Y,Q}(S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V}_{y},\xi):y\in Y)]

\displaystyle\geq 1-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\sim\Xi\\
(Y,P,Q)\sim\mathcal{X}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[|B|^{|Y|}(1-\operatorname{sound}_{Y,Q}(S(\mathbf{V}_{y},\xi):y\in Y))]

by (
39
)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\sim\Xi\\
(Y,P,Q)\sim\mathcal{X}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[O_{\mathbf{B}}(1)(1-\operatorname{sound}_{Y,Q}(S(\mathbf{V}_{y},\xi):y\in Y))]

\mathbf{B}
has bounded arity

\displaystyle=1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(1)\underset{\xi\sim\Xi}{\mathbb{E}}[1-\operatorname{sound}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{B}}(S(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi):x\in X)]

\displaystyle\geq 1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta),

by (
38
)

as desired.
∎

Next, we show that
S_{\operatorname{COR}}
rounds similar local configurations to near-identical outputs.

Lemma 6.35
.

Let
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
be such that
\|S(\mathbf{V})-S(\mathbf{W})\|_{2,2}\leq\eta
. Then,

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\\
{\mathbf{p}\sim\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle[S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V},\xi,\mathbf{p})\neq S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{W},\xi,\mathbf{p})]\leq O_{\mathbf{B}}(\eta).

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\\
{\mathbf{p}\sim\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle[S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V},\xi,\mathbf{p})\neq S_{\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{W},\xi,\mathbf{p})]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\\
{\mathbf{p}\sim\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B}}\end{subarray}}[\operatorname{plur}(S(\mathbf{V},\xi)+\mathbf{p})\neq\operatorname{plur}(S(\mathbf{W},\xi)+\mathbf{p})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}\in B}\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\\
{\mathbf{p}\sim\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B}}\end{subarray}}[\operatorname{plur}(S(\mathbf{V},\xi)+\mathbf{p})=b\wedge\operatorname{plur}(S(\mathbf{W},\xi)+\mathbf{p})=b^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}\in B}\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\\
{\mathbf{p}\sim\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B}}\end{subarray}}[S(\mathbf{V},\xi)_{b}+p_{b}\geq S(\mathbf{V},\xi)_{b^{\prime}}+p_{b^{\prime}}\wedge S(\mathbf{W},\xi)_{b^{\prime}}+p_{b^{\prime}}\geq S(\mathbf{W},\xi)_{b}+p_{b}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}\in B}\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi}\\
{\mathbf{p}\sim[0,1/(2|B|)]^{B}}\end{subarray}}[S(\mathbf{V},\xi)_{b}-S(\mathbf{V},\xi)_{b^{\prime}}\geq p_{b^{\prime}}-p_{b}\geq S(\mathbf{W},\xi)_{b}-S(\mathbf{W},\xi)_{b^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}\in B}\underset{\xi\sim\Xi}{\mathbb{E}}[2|B|\cdot|S(\mathbf{V},\xi)_{b}-S(\mathbf{V},\xi)_{b^{\prime}}-(S(\mathbf{W},\xi)_{b}-S(\mathbf{W},\xi)_{b^{\prime}})|]

\displaystyle(*)

\displaystyle\leq 2|B|^{3}\underset{\xi\sim\Xi}{\mathbb{E}}[\|S(\mathbf{V},\xi)-S(\mathbf{W},\xi)\|_{1}]

\displaystyle(**)

\displaystyle\leq 2|B|^{3}\|S(\mathbf{V})-S(\mathbf{W})\|_{2,2}=O_{\mathbf{B}}(\eta),

where (*) follows from the fact that the CDF of the distribution
p_{b}-p_{b^{\prime}}
is bounded by
2|B|
everywhere and (**) follows from Hölder’s inequality (and the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\Xi}[1]=1
).
∎

6.7
Proof of
Theorem

6.1

We now finally have all the pieces needed to prove
Theorem

6.1
, which we restate for convenience.

See
6.1

Proof.

By
Theorem

6.4
, it suffices to prove that, for every instance
\mathcal{X}
of
\PCSP((\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})+\operatorname{EQ})
with SDP value at least
1-2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}
, there exists an integral assignment
\mathcal{X}\to\mathbf{B}
with soundness at least
1-\beta_{\operatorname{EQ}}/2
.
In particular, we do not need to present an efficient algorithm as that automatically follows from Raghavendra’s theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an arbitrary instance of
\PCSP((\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})+\operatorname{EQ})
and let
\mathcal{V}\in\mathbb{V}^{N}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}+\operatorname{EQ}}
have completeness at least
1-2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}
. Split
\mathcal{X}
into two parts:
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}}
as an instance of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{EQ}}
as an instance of
\PCSP(\operatorname{EQ})
. Let
p\in[0,1]
be the fraction of weight on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}}
. Note that any assignment to the variables that sets all variables to the same value satisfies every constraint in
\mathcal{X}_{EQ}
. Thus, the best integral assignment to
\mathcal{X}
has value at least
1-p
. If
1-p\geq 1-\beta
, we are done, so we may assume that
1-p<1-\beta
(i.e.,
p>\beta>\alpha
). Note further that

1-2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}\leq\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X},\mathbf{A}+\operatorname{EQ}}(\mathcal{V})=p\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})+(1-p)\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{EQ}},\operatorname{EQ}}(\mathcal{V}).

Since
\operatorname{comp}
has range
[0,1]
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}}{p}\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}}{\alpha},

(41)

\displaystyle\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{EQ}},\operatorname{EQ}}(\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}}{1-p}.

(42)

By
Corollary

6.7
, we may pick
\theta=\alpha/2
so that, for any finite set
Z
,
\operatorname{BCR}^{|Z|\cdot|A|+N}_{\theta}
is
(\alpha/2,\beta+\alpha/2)
-robust for
\mathcal{X}
(for sufficiently large
N
). Pick
|Z|\geq 2|X|^{2}/\alpha
and
\delta=\Theta_{\mathbf{A}}(\alpha^{2})
. By
Lemma

6.30
, we have that the collection of rounding schemes
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
is
(\alpha/2-O_{\mathbf{A}}(\sqrt{\delta}),\beta+\alpha/2+\frac{|X|^{2}}{|Z|})
-robust, where we pick
\delta

sufficiently small so that
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
is
(\alpha/4,2\beta)
-robust. Since by (
41
) we have that
\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}},\mathbf{A}}(\mathcal{V})\geq 1-2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}/\alpha\geq 1-\alpha/4
, we have that applying
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
to
\mathbf{V}
satisfies at least
1-2\beta
fraction of the constraints of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}}
.

In particular, when
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}
is applied to
\mathbf{V}
, we satisfy
1-2\beta
fraction of the constraints of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}}
on average. Furthermore, by
Lemma

6.34
,
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}
is
(\alpha/4,\Theta_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta))
-robust for
\mathcal{X}
. So,
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}
when applied to
\mathcal{V}
satisfies
1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta)
-fraction of the constraints of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}}
.

Now, we evaluate the performance of
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}
rounding
\mathcal{V}
for
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{EQ}}
. Fix a constraint
(x,y)
, and assume that
\operatorname{comp}_{\{x,y\},\operatorname{EQ}}(\mathcal{V})=1-\gamma
. Observe that

\operatorname{comp}_{\{x,y\},\operatorname{EQ}}(\mathcal{V})=1-\frac{\|\mathbf{V}_{x}-\mathbf{V}_{y}\|^{2}_{2}}{2},

so
\|\mathbf{V}_{x}-\mathbf{V}_{y}\|^{2}_{2}=2\gamma
. By
Lemma

6.31
with
\eta=\sqrt{2\gamma/\delta}
, we have that

\|\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V}_{x})-\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta}(\mathbf{V}_{y})\|_{2,2}\leq 8\sqrt[4]{\frac{2\gamma}{\delta}}\leq 10\sqrt[4]{\frac{\gamma}{\delta}}.

Thus, by
Lemma

6.35
, we have that

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\xi\sim\Xi^{\operatorname{BCR}}_{|Z|\cdot|A|+N,\theta}}\\
{\mathbf{p}\sim\Xi_{\operatorname{COR},B}}\end{subarray}}[\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V}_{x},\xi,\mathbf{p})\neq\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}(\mathbf{V}_{y},\xi,\mathbf{p})]\leq O_{\mathbf{B}}((\gamma/\delta)^{1/4})=O_{\mathbf{B}}(\gamma^{1/4}\alpha^{-1/2}).

In other words, the probability that
x
and
y
are rounded to the same value by applying
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}
to
\mathcal{V}
is
1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(\gamma^{1/4}\alpha^{-1/2})
. Since this soundness is a convex function of
\gamma
, we have that, if
\operatorname{comp}_{\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{EQ}},\operatorname{EQ}}(\mathcal{V})=1-\lambda
, then
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}
satisfies
1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(\lambda^{1/4}\alpha^{-1/2})
fraction of the equality constraints on average. By (
42
), we have that
\lambda\leq\frac{2\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}}{1-p}
, so the expected number of constraints of
\mathcal{X}
satisfied by applying
\operatorname{REQ}^{N,Z}_{\theta,\delta,\operatorname{COR}}
to
\mathcal{V}
is at least

\displaystyle p(1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta))+(1-p)(1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(\lambda^{1/4}\alpha^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle\geq 1-p\cdot O_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta)-(1-p)O_{\mathbf{B}}\left(\frac{(2\alpha_{EQ})^{1/4}}{(1-p)^{1/4}\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-O_{\mathbf{B}}\left(p\beta+(1-p)^{3/4}\frac{\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-O_{\mathbf{B}}\left(\beta+\frac{\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right),

where the last line bound
p\leq 1
and
(1-p)^{3/4}\leq 1
for any
p\in[0,1]
.
In particular, for
\alpha_{\operatorname{EQ}}=\Theta_{\mathbf{B}}(\alpha^{2}\beta^{4})
, we get
1-O_{\mathbf{B}}(\beta)=1-\beta_{\operatorname{EQ}}
fraction of the constraints are satisfied, as desired.
∎

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we proved a number of new results on the nature of Robust Promise CSPs. First, our integrality gap for
(\textsc{1-in-3-SAT},\textsc{NAE-SAT})
shows that the
(\epsilon,O(\log\log(1/\epsilon)/\log(1/\epsilon)))
-robust algorithm for the
\operatorname{AT}
polymorphism due to Brakensiek–Guruswami–Sandeep
[
BGS25
]
is nearly tight. Second, our algorithms for the Majority, Plurality, and separable polymorphisms show that robust algorithms due to Charikar–Makarychev–Makarychev
[
CMM06
,
CMM09
]
can be extended to quite general settings. Finally, our robust algorithm for a robust PCSP template with equality added shows that a rich theory of gadget reductions between PCSPs can be used in the theory of robust CSPs in a black-box manner. Moving forward, there are a number of directions for further exploration.

•

In
Section

3
, we presented an integrality gap for
\operatorname{fiPCSP}(\textsc{1-in-3-SAT},\textsc{NAE-SAT})
. More generally, can we get an integrality gap for
\operatorname{fiPCSP}(\textsc{$a$-in-$b$-SAT},\textsc{NAE-SAT})
with similar asymptotics? In the case for which
b=2a
, then MAJ are polymorphisms, so in general we consider
a/b\in(0,1)\setminus\{1/2\}
. As far as we know, one cannot use only gadget reductions to establish this broader family of gaps.

•

Recall that Brakensiek, Guruswami, and Sandeep
[
BGS25
]
constructed robust algorithms for all Boolean PCSPs with the MAJ or AT polymorphisms. In
Section

4
and
Section

5
, we greatly improved the analysis and scope of BGS’s MAJ algorithm. What would such a generalization look like for AT? One possible direction is to remove the “
\alpha
-conservative” assumption on the map
\rho
in
Theorem

5.3
.

•

Can one generalize the analysis in
Section

6
to fully resolve
1.4
? With the current techniques, a loss of at least
\epsilon^{1/2}
seems necessary due to the use of the geometry of
L^{2}
-spaces (e.g.,
Proposition

6.32
). Perhaps sharper asymptotics could be achieved by a direct construction of a quasirandom approximate polymorphisms for
\Gamma\cup\{\operatorname{EQ}\}
.

Appendix A
Omitted proofs

In this section, we give the proof of some of the technical results stated in the previous sections of the paper.

A.1
Omitted proofs from
Section

3

We start by formally establishing the first statement in the following theorem (cf. the sketch of the proof given in
Section

3.3
).

See
3.7

We shall use some technical lemmas.

Lemma A.1
.

If
\mathbf{v}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{3}
are unit vectors such that
\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}+\mathbf{v}_{3}=-\mathbf{v}_{0}
then there is a probability distribution
D
supported on the assignments
\{(-1,-1,1),(-1,1,-1),(1,-1,-1)\}
such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D}[x_{i}]=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}
.

Proof.

Let
D
be the probability distribution where we take the assignment
(-1,-1,1)
with probability
\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}
, we take the assignment
(-1,1,-1)
with probability
\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}
, and we take the assignment
(1,-1,-1)
with probability
\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}
. We now make the following observations:

1.

\mathbb{E}_{D}[x_{1}]=\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}-\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}-\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}=\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}-\frac{1+(\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}+\mathbf{v}_{3})\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}=\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{D}[{x_{1}}{x_{2}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}-\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}+\frac{1+\mathbf{v}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}}{2}=\frac{-1+(\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{3})\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}+\mathbf{v}_{3})}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1+\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}+2\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{2}+\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{3}}{2}=\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{2}

The remaining cases follow by symmetry.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Given unit vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{2}
,
\mathbf{v}_{3}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}+\Delta_{i}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}+\Delta_{ij}
.

for some
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{12},\Delta_{13},\Delta_{23}\in\mathbb{R}
, if there is a probability distribution
D
such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D}[x_{i}]=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}
.

then there is a pseudo-distribution
D^{\prime}
(i.e.,
D^{\prime}
may give negative probabilities to some events but the probabilities should still sum to
1
) such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime}}[x_{i}]=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime}}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}
.

3.

For each assignment
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\{-1,1\}^{3}
, the (pseudo-)probabilities of that assignment under
D^{\prime}
and
D
differ by at most
\frac{|\Delta_{1}|+|\Delta_{2}|+|\Delta_{3}|+|\Delta_{12}|+|\Delta_{13}|+|\Delta_{23}|}{8}
.

This follows from the following proposition.

Proposition A.3
.

If
\mathbf{v}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{3}
are unit vectors and
p_{-1,-1,-1}
,
p_{-1,-1,1}
,
p_{-1,1,-1}
,
p_{-1,1,1}
,
p_{1,-1,-1}
,
p_{1,-1,1}
,
p_{1,1,-1}
, and
p_{1,1,1}
are real numbers such that

1.

p_{1,-1,-1}+p_{1,-1,1}+p_{1,1,-1}+p_{1,1,1}+p_{-1,-1,-1}+p_{-1,-1,1}+p_{-1,1,-1}+p_{-1,1,1}=1

2.

\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p_{1,-1,-1}+p_{1,-1,1}+p_{1,1,-1}+p_{1,1,1}-p_{-1,-1,-1}-p_{-1,-1,1}-p_{-1,1,-1}-p_{-1,1,1}
.

3.

\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p_{-1,1,-1}+p_{-1,1,1}+p_{1,1,-1}+p_{1,1,1}-p_{-1,-1,-1}-p_{-1,-1,1}-p_{1,-1,-1}-p_{1,-1,1}
.

4.

\mathbf{v}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p_{-1,-1,1}+p_{-1,1,1}+p_{1,-1,1}+p_{1,1,1}-p_{-1,-1,-1}-p_{-1,1,-1}-p_{1,-1,-1}-p_{1,1,-1}
.

5.

\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{2}=p_{-1,-1,-1}+p_{-1,-1,1}+p_{1,1,-1}+p_{1,1,1}-p_{-1,1,-1}-p_{-1,1,1}-p_{1,-1,-1}-p_{1,-1,1}
.

6.

\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{3}=p_{-1,-1,-1}+p_{-1,1,-1}+p_{1,-1,1}+p_{1,1,1}-p_{-1,-1,1}-p_{-1,1,1}-p_{1,-1,-1}-p_{1,1,-1}
.

7.

\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{3}=p_{-1,-1,-1}+p_{1,-1,-1}+p_{-1,1,1}+p_{1,1,1}-p_{-1,-1,1}-p_{1,-1,1}-p_{-1,1,-1}-p_{1,1,-1}
.

and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
are unit vectors such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}+\Delta_{i}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}+\Delta_{ij}
.

for some
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{12},\Delta_{13},\Delta_{23}\in\mathbb{R}
then if we take

1.

p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}=p_{-1,-1,-1}+\frac{-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{12}+\Delta_{13}+\Delta_{23}}{8}

2.

p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}=p_{-1,-1,1}+\frac{-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{12}-\Delta_{13}-\Delta_{23}}{8}

3.

p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}=p_{-1,1,-1}+\frac{-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{12}+\Delta_{13}-\Delta_{23}}{8}

4.

p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}=p_{1,-1,-1}+\frac{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{12}-\Delta_{13}+\Delta_{23}}{8}

5.

p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}=p_{-1,1,1}+\frac{-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{12}-\Delta_{13}+\Delta_{23}}{8}

6.

p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}=p_{1,-1,1}+\frac{\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{12}+\Delta_{13}-\Delta_{23}}{8}

7.

p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}=p_{1,1,-1}+\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{12}-\Delta_{13}-\Delta_{23}}{8}

8.

p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}=p_{1,1,1}+\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{12}+\Delta_{13}+\Delta_{23}}{8}

we will have that

1.

p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}=1

2.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}
.

3.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}
.

4.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

5.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}
.

6.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

7.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

Proof.

This follows from the following observations:

1.

The
\pm\frac{\Delta_{i}}{8}
terms contribute
\Delta_{i}
to the sum for
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
and cancel out for the other sums.

2.

Similarly, the
\pm\frac{\Delta_{ij}}{8}
terms contribute
\Delta_{ij}
to the sum for
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}
and cancel out for the other sums. ∎

Lemma A.4
.

If
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
are unit vectors and
D^{\prime}
is a pseudo-distribution such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime}}[x_{i}]=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime}}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}
.

then if we take
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\sqrt{1-{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}+{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{2}=\sqrt{1-{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}+{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{3}=\sqrt{1-{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}+{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}_{3}
where
\mathbf{z}_{1}
,
\mathbf{z}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{z}_{3}
are orthogonal to
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
, and each other for some
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
and take
D^{\prime\prime}
to be the pseudo-distribution where the probability that each assignment occurs under
D^{\prime\prime}
is
(1-\epsilon^{\prime})
times the probability that the assignment occurs under
D^{\prime}
plus
\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
then

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime\prime}}[x_{i}]=\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
.

This follows from the following proposition.

Proposition A.5
.

If
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
are unit vectors and
p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}
,
p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}
,
p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}
,
p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}
,
p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}
,
p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}
,
p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}
, and
p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}
are real numbers (not necessarily non-negative) such that

1.

p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}=1

2.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}
.

3.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}
.

4.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

5.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}
.

6.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

7.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}=p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}-p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

then if we take
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\sqrt{1-{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}+{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{2}=\sqrt{1-{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}+{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{3}=\sqrt{1-{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}+{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}_{3}
where
\mathbf{z}_{1}
,
\mathbf{z}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{z}_{3}
are orthogonal to
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
, and each other then if we take
p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
,
p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
,
p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
,
p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{-1,1,1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
,
p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
,
p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{1,-1,1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
,
p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{1,1,-1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
, and
p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}=(1-\epsilon^{\prime})p^{\prime}_{1,1,1}+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
then

1.

p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}=1

2.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}
.

3.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}
.

4.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

5.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{2}=p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}
.

6.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{3}=p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

7.

\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{3}=p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,-1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,1}+p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,-1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,-1,1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{-1,1,-1}-p^{\prime\prime}_{1,1,-1}
.

Proof.

This follows from the following observations:

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}=(1-{\epsilon^{\prime}})\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime\prime}_{j}=(1-{\epsilon^{\prime}})\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}
.

3.

In each of the above sums, the
\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
terms cancel out as assigning probability
\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{8}
to each of the possible assignments does not contribute anything to the biases or pairwise biases. ∎

Proof of the first statement of Theorem
3.7
:

To prove the first statement of Theorem
3.7
, we observe that for each triple of vectors
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
sampled from
T^{\prime}_{x}
or
T^{\prime}_{-x}
that is not discarded, there is a triple of unit vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{3}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}+\mathbf{v}_{3}=\pm\mathbf{v}_{0}
and for all
i\in[3]
,
||\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}||}-\mathbf{v}_{i}||\leq\frac{\epsilon}{100}
.

To see this, observe that when we sample
\mathbf{w}
and
\mathbf{u}
from

\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)^{d}
,
we can take

1.

\mathbf{v}_{1}=bx\mathbf{v}_{0}+\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{1-(2|x|-1)^{2}}}{2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\frac{\mathbf{w}}{||\mathbf{w}||}+\sqrt{1-\frac{1-(2|x|-1)^{2}}{4(1-x^{2})}}\frac{\mathbf{u}}{||\mathbf{u}||}\right)
,

2.

\mathbf{v}_{2}=bx\mathbf{v}_{0}+\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{1-(2|x|-1)^{2}}}{2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\frac{\mathbf{w}}{||\mathbf{w}||}-\sqrt{1-\frac{1-(2|x|-1)^{2}}{4(1-x^{2})}}\frac{\mathbf{u}}{||\mathbf{u}||}\right)
,

3.

\mathbf{v}_{3}=b(1-2x)\mathbf{v}_{0}+\sqrt{1-(2|x|-1)^{2}}\frac{\mathbf{w}}{||\mathbf{w}||}
,

where
b\in\{-1,1\}
. The adjustments we need to shift from
\mathbf{v}_{i}
to
\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}||}
are as follows:

1.

||w||
and
||u||
may differ from
1
by up to
\frac{\epsilon}{1000}
.

2.

We need to shift from the vectors we obtain based on
\mathbf{w}
and
\mathbf{u}
to the centers
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
of the regions they are contained in.

3.

We need to rescale
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{3}
.

It is not hard to show that these adjustments have a total length of at most
\frac{\epsilon}{100}
.

A similar argument applies for the triples of vectors
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
where
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
is sampled from
A^{\prime}_{-x_{0}}
and the triples of vectors
(-\mathbf{v}_{0},-\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
where
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
is sampled from
A^{\prime}_{x_{0}}
. For these triples, we use
(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3})=(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{0},-\mathbf{v}_{0})
and
(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3})==(-\mathbf{v}_{0},-\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{0})
respectively.

The first statement now follows by applying Lemmas
A.1
,
A.2
, and
A.4
. For each triple that is not discarded, letting
\mathbf{v}_{1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{3}
be the vectors from the argument above, by Lemma
A.1
, there is a distribution
D
supported on satisfying assignments such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D}[x_{i}]=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}
.

By Lemma
A.2
, there is a pseudo-distribution
D^{\prime}
such that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime}}[x_{i}]=\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}||}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime}}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}||}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}||}
.

3.

For each assignment
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\{-1,1\}^{3}
, the (pseudo-)probabilities of this assignment under
D
and
D^{\prime}
differ by at most
\frac{\epsilon}{50}
.

By Lemma
A.4
, Letting
D^{\prime\prime}
be the probability distribution where if an assignment has pseudo-probability
p
under
D^{\prime}
then it has probability
(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4})p+\frac{\epsilon}{32}
under
D^{\prime\prime}
, we have that

1.

For all
i\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime\prime}}[x_{i}]=\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}}\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}||}+\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2}\mathbf{z}_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}}\right)\cdot\mathbf{v}_{0}
.

2.

For all distinct
i,j\in[3]
,
\mathbb{E}_{D^{\prime\prime}}[{x_{i}}{x_{j}}]=\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}}\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{i}||}+\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2}\mathbf{z}_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}}\right)\cdot\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}}\frac{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}}{||\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}||}+\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2}\mathbf{z}_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{j}}\right)
. ∎

We now prove Lemma
3.9
.

See
3.9

Proof.

For the first statement, writing
||\mathbf{w}||^{2}-1=\sum_{j=1}^{d}{(w_{j}^{2}-\frac{1}{d})}
where each
w_{j}
is drawn independently from
N(0,\frac{1}{d})
, since

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta(w_{j}^{2}-\frac{1}{d})}\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{2{\pi}}}e^{-\frac{d{w}^{2}}{2}}e^{\beta(w^{2}-\frac{1}{d})}dw}=\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-2\beta}}e^{-\frac{\beta}{d}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{2}}dz}=\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-2\beta}}e^{-\frac{\beta}{d}}

whenever
|\beta|<\frac{d}{2}
, if we let
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{\beta}{d}
then we have that

P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}{\left({w_{j}}^{2}-\frac{1}{d}\right)}\geq t\right)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta\sum_{j=1}^{d}{({w_{j}}^{2}-\frac{1}{d})}}\right]}{e^{{\beta}t}}\leq e^{-d({\beta^{\prime}}t+\beta^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}ln(1-2\beta^{\prime}))}

and

P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}{\left({w_{j}}^{2}-\frac{1}{d}\right)}\leq-t\right)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta\sum_{j=1}^{d}{({w_{j}}^{2}-\frac{1}{d})}}\right]}{e^{-{\beta}t}}\leq e^{-d(-{\beta^{\prime}}t+\beta^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}ln(1-2\beta^{\prime}))}.

whenever
|\beta^{\prime}|<\frac{1}{2}
. Since
\ln(1-x)=-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{x^{j}}{j}}
, if
|x|\leq\frac{1}{2}
then
\ln(1-x)\geq-x-x^{2}
so setting
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{t}{4}
, we have that
P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}{\left({w_{j}}^{2}-\frac{1}{d}\right)}\geq t\right)\leq e^{-\frac{dt^{2}}{8}}
.
Similarly, setting
\beta^{\prime}=-\frac{t}{4}
, we have that
P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}{\left({w_{j}}^{2}-\frac{1}{d}\right)}\leq-t\right)\leq e^{-\frac{dt^{2}}{8}}
.

For the second statement, observe that for all
\beta\geq 0
, since
\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{u}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}{{w_{j}}{u_{j}}}
and

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta{w_{j}}{u_{j}}}\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{2{\pi}}}e^{-\frac{d{u}^{2}}{2}}e^{\beta{w_{j}}u}du}=e^{\frac{{\beta}^{2}{w_{j}^{2}}}{2d}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\left(z-\frac{{\beta}w_{j}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{2}}{2}}dz}=e^{\frac{{\beta}^{2}{w_{j}^{2}}}{2d}}

we have that for all
t\geq 0
,

P(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{u}\geq t)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{d}{{w_{j}}u_{j}}}\right]}{e^{{\beta}t}}=e^{\frac{{\beta}^{2}{||w||^{2}}}{2d}-{\beta}t}
. Plugging in
\beta=\frac{td}{||w||^{2}}
, we obtain that

P(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{u}\geq t)\leq 2e^{-\frac{d{t^{2}}}{2||\mathbf{w}||^{2}}}
. By symmetry,
P(\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{u}\leq-t)\leq 2e^{-\frac{d{t^{2}}}{2||\mathbf{w}||^{2}}}
.
∎

A.2
Omitted proofs from
Section

6

We shall prove the following.

See
6.17

Proof.

First we prove that (1) implies (2). To see why, by (1), we have that for all
\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}
, there exists
\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})-\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}).

(43)

Observe, using (
29
), that
\Tr(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}))=\Tr(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W}))=\Tr(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}))=|Y|
for all
\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}
.
Thus, the trace of (
43
) implies that
\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}=0
.
Hence,
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})-\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{W})\in K_{Y,A}^{0}
, as desired.

We next focus on proving (1). We start by giving a self-contained proof, and then we show how the same result can be derived from a result on the projection of hypermatrices.

Let
K_{Y,A}^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{(Y\times A)^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
be the dual linear space of
K_{Y,A}
. It suffices to show for every
M\in K_{Y,A}^{*}
that
M(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V}))=0
. We can parameterize each element
M\in K_{Y,A}
as a list of
|Y|^{2}
matrices
(M^{y,y^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{A\times A}:y,y^{\prime}\in Y)
such that, for all
\mathbf{a}\in A
, we have that the condition
M(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{a}))
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{y,y^{\prime}\in Y}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a_{y},a_{y^{\prime}}}=0.

(44)

We now see to generalize (
44
) as follows.

Claim A.6
.

For any list of vectors
(\mathbf{u}_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}:y\in Y)
for which there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
y\in Y
,
\sum_{a\in A}u_{y,a}=c
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a}u_{y,a}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\mathbf{u}_{y}^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}_{y^{\prime}}=0.

(45)

Proof.

Assume for now that
c=1
. For each
a\in A
, let
\mathbf{e}_{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
be the vector such that
\mathbf{e}_{a,a^{\prime}}=\mathbf{1}[a=a^{\prime}]
for all
a^{\prime}\in A
. Then, for all
\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}
, (
44
) can be reinterpreted as

\sum_{y\in Y}M^{y,y}_{a_{y},a_{y}}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\mathbf{e}_{a_{y}}^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{a_{y^{\prime}}}=0.

For each
y\in Y
, observe that
\mathbf{u}_{y}=\sum_{a\in A}u_{y,a}\mathbf{e}_{a}
. Now, for all
\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}
, let
\lambda_{\mathbf{a}}=\prod_{y\in Y}u_{y,a_{y}}
. Then, we can see that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}}\prod_{z\in Y}u_{z,a_{z}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\end{subarray}}M^{y,y}_{a_{y},a_{y}}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\mathbf{e}_{a_{y}}^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{a_{y^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y}_{a,a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}\\
a_{y}=a\end{subarray}}\prod_{z\in Y}u_{z,a_{z}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y\\
a,a^{\prime}\in A\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{a}^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{a^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in A^{Y}\\
a_{y}=a\\
a_{y^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{z\in Y}u_{z,a_{z}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y}_{a,a}u_{y,a}\cdot\prod_{z\in Y\setminus\{y\}}\sum_{a_{z}\in A}u_{z,a_{z}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y\\
a,a^{\prime}\in A\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{a}^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{a^{\prime}}\cdot u_{y,a}u_{y^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{z\in Y\setminus\{y,y^{\prime}\}}\sum_{a_{z}\in A}u_{z,a_{z}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y}_{a,a}u_{y,a}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y\\
a,a^{\prime}\in A\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{a}^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{a^{\prime}}\cdot u_{y,a}u_{y^{\prime},a^{\prime}}

(
c=1
)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a}u_{y,a}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\mathbf{u}_{y}^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{u}_{y^{\prime}},

as desired. Now consider
c\neq 1
. If
c\neq 0
we can rescale
(\mathbf{u}_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}:y\in Y)
so that
c=1
. If
c=0
, we can construct a sequence of sequences
\{(\mathbf{u}^{(i)}_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}:y\in Y):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
approaching
(\mathbf{u}_{y}:y\in Y)
in the
\ell_{2}
norm, but each
c_{i}:=\sum_{a\in A}u^{(i)}_{y,a}
is nonzero. Then, we get (
45
) in the limit.
∎

For all
i\in[N]
and
y\in Y
, let
\mathbf{z}^{i,y}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
satisfying
z^{i,y}_{a}=v_{y,a,i}
, where we recall that
\mathbf{v}_{y,a}
is the
a
-th vector defining
\mathbf{V}_{y}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A}
. Since
\sum_{a\in A}\mathbf{v}_{y,a}=\mathbf{v}_{0}
for all
y\in Y
, we have for all
y\in Y
and
i\in[N]
that
\sum_{a\in A}z^{i,y}_{a}=v_{0,i}
. Assume that
v_{0,i}\neq 0
for all
i\in[N]
. Then, apply
A.6
with
(z^{i,y}/v_{0,i}:y\in Y)
for all
i\in[N]
to get that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i,0}^{2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a}\frac{z^{i,y}_{a}}{v_{0,i}}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\frac{1}{v_{i,0}^{2}}(\mathbf{z}^{i,y})^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}^{i,y^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a}\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{y,a,i}v_{0,i}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\mathbf{z}^{i,y})^{T}M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{z}^{i,y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a}\langle\mathbf{v}_{y,a},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in A}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{y,a,i}v_{y^{\prime},a^{\prime},i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in Y\\
a\in A\end{subarray}}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a}\langle\mathbf{v}_{y,a},\mathbf{v}_{y,a}\rangle+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in Y}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in A}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a^{\prime}}\langle\mathbf{v}_{y,a},\mathbf{v}_{y^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{y,y^{\prime}\in Y}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in A}M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,a^{\prime}}\langle\mathbf{v}_{y,a},\mathbf{v}_{y^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle=M(\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})),

as desired, so
\mathcal{V}\in K_{Y,A}
.
∎

Alternative proof of
Lemma

6.17
(1).

We shall use a result on the projection of hypermatrices proved in
[
CŽ23a
]
. For
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
, consider a set
\mathcal{M}=\{M^{y,y^{\prime}}:1\leq y<y^{\prime}\leq q\}
of
n\times n
matrices with integral entries. We say that
\mathcal{M}
is a
realisable
system if the matrices of
\mathcal{M}
are the 2-dimensional projections of a unique hypermatrix; formally, if
there exists a hypermatrix
\Lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{\tiny\underbrace{n\times n\times\dots\times n}_{q}}
such that

\displaystyle M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,b}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in[n]^{q}\\
z_{y}=a,\,z_{y^{\prime}}=b\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{z}}

for each
M^{y,y^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{M}
and each
a,b\in[n]
. We say that
\mathcal{M}
is a
realistic
system if its matrices are locally compatible in the following sense: For each
1\leq y_{1}<y_{2}\leq q
, each
1\leq w_{1}<w_{2}\leq q
, and each
r,s\in[2]
such that
y_{r}=w_{s}
, it holds that

\displaystyle(M^{y_{1},y_{2}})^{\circ r}\mathbf{1}=(M^{w_{1},w_{2}})^{\circ s}\mathbf{1},

where we use the notation
M^{\circ 1}\coloneqq M
and
M^{\circ 2}\coloneqq M^{\top}
. We will use the following result.

Theorem A.7

(
[
CŽ23a
]
)
.

A system
\mathcal{M}
is realistic if and only if it is realisable.
19
19

19

This result was proved in
[
CŽ23a
]
in the case of arbitrarily dimensional projections and hypermatrices that are not necessarily cubic (i.e., whose modes have possibly distinct lengths). Here we only need the restricted case described above.

Take now some global configuration
\mathcal{V}\in(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{Y}
, and let
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})\in\mathbb{R}^{(Y\times A)^{2}}
be the corresponding Gram matrix. Suppose first that
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})
has rational entries, and let
N
be a common denominator. Denote by
M^{y,y^{\prime}}
the
(y,y^{\prime})
-th block of
N\cdot\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})
(of size
A\times A
), and observe that the quantity

\displaystyle(M^{y,y^{\prime}}\mathbf{1})_{a}=N\sum_{b\in A}\langle\mathbf{v}_{y,a},\mathbf{v}_{y^{\prime},b}\rangle=N\langle\mathbf{v}_{y,a},\mathbf{v}_{0}\rangle

is independent on
y^{\prime}
for each
a\in A
. Hence, the system
\mathcal{M}=\{M^{y,y^{\prime}}:1\leq y<y^{\prime}\leq|Y|\}
is realistic. By
Theorem

A.7
, it follows that
\mathcal{M}
is realisable. Let
\Lambda
be its realisation hypermatrix. We claim that
\frac{1}{N}\Lambda
witnesses that
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})
lies in
K_{Y,A}
. I.e., we claim that
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})=\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in A^{Y}}\frac{1}{N}\Lambda_{\mathbf{z}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{z})
. Indeed, for each
1\leq y<y^{\prime}\leq|Y|
and each
a,b\in A
we have

\displaystyle N\cdot\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})_{(y,a),(y^{\prime},b)}=M^{y,y^{\prime}}_{a,b}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in A^{|Y|}\\
z_{y}=a,\,z_{y^{\prime}}=b\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{z}}=\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in A^{|Y|}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{z}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{z})_{(y,a),(y^{\prime},b)}=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in A^{|Y|}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{z}}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbf{z})\right)_{(y,a),(y^{\prime},b)}

as needed. Since
M^{y^{\prime},y}=(M^{y,y^{\prime}})^{\top}
and since the diagonal blocks of the Gram matrix of any global configuration are completely determined by its off-diagonal blocks, the result holds for each
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
, thus proving the claim. Finally, the rational subset of Gram matrices of global configurations in
(\mathbb{S}^{N}_{A})^{Y}
is dense, so the same result must hold for non-rational
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathcal{V})
as well, thus concluding the proof.
∎
